# Bernard Picart: Study Notes

**Site:** Bernard Picart (11 June 1673, Paris - 8 May 1733, Amsterdam), French draughtsman, engraver and book illustrator working in Amsterdam, the son of Etienne Picart, called le Romain; sent to the Academie royale in 1689, in Antwerp in 1696, in Amsterdam from 1711, where he ran an engraving school and a print shop and made the book the visual encyclopaedia of the world -- his Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses de tous les peuples du monde (1723-1743, with the publisher Jean Frederic Bernard) put the world's religions into print in 266 engravings (EN Wikipedia's count for one edition; other counts 243, 250+, 263 -- see leg 1 F8); also the Temple of the Muses (1733) and Impostures innocentes (1734). His widow's inventory, made up 17 April 1736 (not 12 March, as EN Wikipedia states -- see leg 1 F11), listed about 400 portfolios of copperplates and 54 paintings; the Rijksmuseum website lists more than 2,000 works by him (Wikipedia's figure, not verified live in this study -- see leg 1 F24). No words BY Picart survive as a body of writing except his joint declaration with Prosper Marchand on joining the Walloon church (Q12) and his self-styled signature as "barbouilleur et enlumineur" of the Knights of Jubilation (Q14); his defence of reproductive engraving, the Discours sur les prejuges de certains curieux touchant la gravure (1732), is known through the posthumous Impostures innocentes. His life is known chiefly through the Eloge historique written by his widow Anna Vincent, undoubtedly with the help of Prosper Marchand, and through the specialist biography site bernardpicart.net, which is heavily footnoted to the Eloge, Van Eeghen, the Cronstrom/Tessin letters and the Amsterdam archives.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Reinier Vinkeles study: Vinkeles spent forty-three years directing a drawing academy that taught from books like the Teekenboek, engraving the city, the theatre and the Revolution into copper for the Amsterdam book trade; now meet the Frenchman whose Amsterdam engraving school set the template that trade worked inside -- the master who turned the book into a visual encyclopaedia, assembled from second-hand reports into a single consistent engraved system, and whose pupils' pupils taught Vinkeles's master. The doorway is the French engraver who taught Tanje his trade and put the world's religions into print from Amsterdam.

One-hundred-and-sixty-fifth doorway check in a row. D1 "Bernard Picart (1673-1733)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (EN Wikipedia Bernard Picart, 208 lines, read end to end by legs 1, 2 and 3; bernardpicart.net; the birth date rests on the Eloge historique, the certificate not found -- see leg 1 F1). D2 "French draughtsman, engraver and book illustrator working in Amsterdam" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter. D3 "son of Etienne Picart, called le Romain" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (Etienne is le Romain, for his Roman sojourn; Bernard never went to Italy but used "le Romain" in imprints -- leg 1 F1). D4 "taught by Charles Le Brun" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (the Eloge/Wikipedia tradition puts him in Le Brun's circle, introduced by his father; but no documented master-pupil prize -- the specialist site flags the prize story as a misunderstanding, Le Brun's last ceremony 24 September 1689, Picart not listed as a winner; his documented Academie teacher was Sebastien Leclerc -- leg 1 F2). D5 "received into the Academie royale in 1689" -- FAILS in substance and in letter (what is documented is that he was sent to the Academie in 1689 to learn drawing under Leclerc; formal reception as a member is not established in any source read -- leg 1 F17). D6 "Antwerp 1696; settled in Amsterdam 1711" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (left Paris end September 1696, winter in Antwerp, Prix du dessein; The Hague January 1710, Amsterdam 1711 -- leg 1 F7). D7 "Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses de tous les peuples du monde (1723-1743, with the publisher Jean Frederic Bernard; 266 engravings, 10 volumes)" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (the work, the publisher and the first volumes 1723 hold; but 266 engravings and 10 volumes are EN Wikipedia's count for one edition -- other serious sources give 243, 250+, 263 plates and 7, 11 or 12 volumes, and dates 1723-1737 as well as 1723-1743 -- leg 1 F8). D8 "also the Temple of the Muses (1733) and Impostures innocentes (1734)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (Dutch Temple 1733; Impostures 1734, widow's imprint, Met/RCT -- the 1738 date in FR Wikipedia is the outlier). D9 "He ran an engraving school: pupils included Jacob Folkema, Jakob van der Schley, Pieter Tanje, Francois Morellon la Cave and Johann Philipp Endelich" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter for the five named (EN Wikipedia roster; RKD for Endelich), with the roster caveat (FR Wikipedia lists further, likely Paris-period, names; the two rosters are not reconciled -- leg 1 F9). D10 "His widow's inventory of 12 March 1736 listed about 400 portfolios of copperplates and 54 paintings" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (the counts hold across both sources; but the estate inventory was made up on 17 April 1736 per the specialist site citing the Amsterdam notarial archive, not 12 March -- leg 1 F11). D11 "the Rijksmuseum holds 2,000+ of his works" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (Wikipedia's figure, "more than 2,000 works online"; no Rijksmuseum object page was opened directly by any leg, so the count was not verified live -- leg 1 F24). D12 "He is the top pick because he is the trunk of the Amsterdam engraving line" and "Studying him first unlocks the next three queued names at once (Folkema and Schley are his pupils, Dubourg his 1729 collaborator)" -- interpretive; HOLDS in substance. "VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero '## Study #' headers; 2 whole-log mentions, both in the #542 entry" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (zero study headers holds; no local file holds; never carried as a subject holds; but the whole-log mentions are now five, not two -- the #543 entry added three: the Depth line, the Push line and the Future-candidates line, all carries, not studies; see C546). "Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3 of #542 (209 lines) and leg 3 of #543 (208 lines -- see C540)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (208 lines in legs 1, 2 and 3 of this run; #542's 209 is the counting-rule difference, see C540/C551). The check is recorded as a HOLD on the substance -- the one-hundred-and-eighteenth hold in the streak -- and a FAIL on the letter -- the forty-first non-hold -- and the streak that began at #420 now stands at one hundred and eighteen holds and forty-one non-holds. (Counting note: the doorway checks number 165 in total; the hold tally counts only since #420, which is why 118 + 41 does not sum to 165.) The letter fails on D4, D5, D7, D10, D11 and D12; the substance fails on D5 alone.

**Depth:** three research legs, no parent page read end to end. ## The life

Bernard Picart was born in Paris on 11 June 1673, between 22:00 and 23:00, in the rue Saint-Jacques (bernardpicart.net). His father was Etienne Picart (1632-1721), graveur du roi, called **le Romain** because he had spent time in Rome and to distinguish him from the other Picarts; his mother was Angelique Tournant. The nickname "le Romain" belongs properly to the father -- "This suffix also occurs sometimes with the name of his son Bernard, although Bernard has never been to Italy" -- yet Bernard himself used it in imprints, e.g. Gemmae antiquae caelatae "chez Bernard Picart le Romain, Amsterdam, 1724". The birth certificate has, as far as the specialist site knows, not yet been found; the birth date rests on the Eloge historique (leg 1 F1).

His first instructions came from his father and from Benoit Audran the elder (1661-1721), whose drawings the boy mimicked from about age eleven or twelve -- Audran was then lodging with the Picarts and sketching in the evenings. In 1689 his father sent him to the Academie royale de peinture et de sculpture to master drawing, under Sebastien Leclerc (1637-1714), who taught him perspective and architecture. The Eloge historique says he won an Academy prize two years later, received from the hands of Charles le Brun -- but the specialist site flags this as a misunderstanding: the last prize ceremony by Le Brun took place on 24 September 1689, Picart is not mentioned as a prize winner in the Academy's documents, and the prize drawing is not known. EN Wikipedia's wording -- "He was taught by Charles le Brun, along with Benoit Audran the Elder, Sebastien Leclerc and Antoine Coypel" -- should therefore be read as "taught in Le Brun's orbit / introduced to Le Brun's circle", not as a documented master-pupil prize. His father introduced him to Academie painters Charles de Lafosse, Rene Antoine Houasse, Jean Jouvenet and Antoine Coypel; the honorary academician Roger de Piles (1635-1709) was of particular importance (leg 1 F2; see C542).

At first Picart "had little sense of making prints, which required a lot of time and patience. He rather wanted to become a draughtsman or a painter, but because his father already owned a successful workshop for printmaking, he eventually decided to follow in his footsteps. This must have been around 1693, because Picart's first signed print dates from that year (Hermaphrodite after Poussin)". His first assignment of which he was proud was the tomb of Cardinal Richelieu by Girardon: four drawings and engravings of different views of the monument. He made two drawings but they could not agree on the price; Girardon gave the first two to the celebrated engraver Charles Simonneau, then came back to Picart because his work was the most satisfying -- and Picart signed his two as both designer and engraver, a signature Simonneau then insisted on claiming for his own two as well, and Picart consented. The four drawings were made "au pinceau avec de la sanguine detrempee" -- brush with diluted red chalk. Already here: the signature as a contested professional claim, and the draughtsman asserting authorship over the engraver.

At the end of September 1696 Picart left for the Dutch Republic "for unknown reasons". He spent the winter in Antwerp, where he won the Prix du dessein of the Koninklijke Academie voor Schone Kunsten; nothing is known about the circumstances or the prize-winning drawing, and its whereabouts are unclear. From spring 1697 until December 1698 he stayed in Amsterdam, then returned to Paris because of his mother's death and his father's illness. It has been suggested he went to England; the specialist site calls this "a mistake based on an account in a letter of September 1698 written by Daniel Cronstrom to Nicodeme Tessin le Jeune" -- "Il est en Angleterre" -- and notes that had he been in England it would have been mentioned in the Eloge (leg 1 F3).

Running alongside was a long negotiation with the Swedish court. From 1696 to 1698 and again from 1709 to 1719, the Swedish architect Nicodemus Tessin Jr. and the diplomat Daniel Cronstrom corresponded about bringing skilled printmakers to Sweden. In September 1696 Cronstrom wrote that two printmakers were eligible: Bernard Picart and Benoit Audran -- "Picart was praised for his drawings, but Benoit had already become more famous as a printmaker". Cronstrom's fuller judgement: "Le Picard est plus routine dans le dessein des figures a la Vandermeule que l'autre; pour la graveure, Mr. Audran a un peu plus d'acquit, mais le Piccard est si fort employe aussi que sa reputation et son scavoir augmentent tous les jours" -- see Q13. Nothing came of it in the 1690s, perhaps because of his father's illness in 1698.

He married Claudine Prost on 23 April 1702 (EN Wikipedia spells her "Cloudina Pros" -- see leg 1 F4). The amateur and critic Roger de Piles became godfather of their first son, "but sadly the baby boy died soon"; a red-chalk portrait of that son, Roger Bernard Picart aged six months, drawn in 1703, survives in the Rijksmuseum (RP-T-1951-75). In 1708 his wife also died, "after which he decided to leave France". The Eloge says "sa femme & ses enfans etant morts" -- wife and children, plural -- but only one son is documented; a Cronstrom letter of 1 July 1709 mentions Picart wanting to take his whole family to Sweden, "consisting of his old father and a little son", and the specialist site notes "So far, I have not found a source referring to this particular son" (leg 1 F5).

Two circumstances pushed him out, in the specialist site's reading: "Crucial was his criticism of certain rituals in the Roman church, but also the fact that in France at that moment there was little interesting work for (reproductive) printmakers". During his first Dutch stay (1697-1698) he had read a dissertation by the Protestant minister Jean Claude (1619-1687) on the Eucharist; back in Paris he read more, talked with an unnamed abbot -- putting the reformers' arguments only in the third person so as not to alarm him -- and met a bookseller with similar ideas, "who lent him the books he needed": Prosper Marchand, who became his closest friend. Both considered leaving France. The Eloge explains at length that Picart used the Swedish job as a pretext: in a letter of 1 July 1709 Cronstrom reported Picart still wanted to leave for Sweden, would travel at his own expense and ask no wages until the return of King Charles XII or the peace. Just before the passport was delivered, police lieutenant Rene d'Argenson -- who had directed the destruction of the Jansenist monastery of Port-Royal in 1709 -- was alarmed that Picart was using the Swedish job as an excuse to leave because of his new faith; the king prohibited his departure. Picart protested, showed his work -- including the Cachet de Michel-Ange, fresh from the press, a shrewd choice because d'Argenson belonged to the Academie des Inscriptions et Belles-Lettres, where numismatics and carved stones were studied -- and showed papers proving he was a serious Catholic; he got a passport valid for only one week, and he and his father Etienne departed "with all speed on January 8, 1710". Through Antwerp they travelled to Rotterdam, where the Sweden trip could begin -- but "the political situation was so precarious that Picart decided to stay in the Republic". The specialist site is careful: "Although Picart wanted the French government to believe differently, his protestant sympathies must have been a stimulus to leave France. But, as said before, Parisian printmakers couldn't easily get a job at the beginning of the eighteenth century... The successful career that Picart expected to meet in Sweden or in the Republic has certainly influenced his decision". He preferred a more tolerant living environment, "but there is no indication that he cherished negative feelings about his French background".

In the first year, 1710-1711, Picart and Marchand rented a building in the Hoogstraat in The Hague, where they held a book and print shop. Marchand had left Paris for The Hague in 1709 because of anti-Protestant threats. On 14 April 1710 they signed up in the Walloon church, declaring (quoted in full at Q12) that God had shown them the errors of the Roman religion and that they had renounced "ses assemblees et a son culte superstitieux et idolatre". On 24 November 1710 Picart joined friends in The Hague for the general chapter of the self-styled "Knights of Jubilation" (chevaliers de la jubilation), signing as "Bernard Picart, barbouilleur et enlumineur de l'ordre" (dauber and illuminator) and Marchand as secretary; the society's meeting place was called Gaillardin (playful cheerfulness). The specialist site notes Berkvens-Stevelinck "shows convincingly that this company has nothing to do with Freemasonry as Jacob suggests" (leg 1 F6).

Marchand left for Amsterdam between December 1710 and April 1711, and Picart soon followed -- "The exact reason is not known, but it seems likely that both men moved to Amsterdam because this city was the most important centre of the book and print trade in the eighteenth century Republic". From 1711 to 1712 he lived in the Nes; from May 1713 on the Singel over the Appelmarkt opposite the old Lutheran church, with the sign L'etoile; later at Singel 434, on the corner of Beulingstraat (H No. 16), where he lived with his family until his death. This is the settlement-date knot: sources saying "settled in Amsterdam in 1710" (Encyclopedia.com; Sotheby's) conflate the Hague arrival with the Amsterdam move; the archival sequence is The Hague January 1710, Amsterdam 1711 (leg 1 F7; see C550).

On 9 September 1712 Picart and Anna Vincent (1684-1736) gave notice of their intended marriage; on 25 September 1712 the marriage took place in Haarlem. Anna's father, the wealthy paper dealer Ysbrand Vincent (1641-1718), was initially opposed; her mother Anne Yver complained repeatedly about his stubborn disapproval in letters to Mrs Moretus in Antwerp. Ysbrand eventually reconciled, and "Undoubtedly through his mediation Picart received the assignment to design title prints/frontispieces for plays performed by the classicist-oriented art society Nil Volentibus Arduum, in which Vincent was closely involved". EN Wikipedia says "In April 1712 he married Anna Vincent... in Haarlem" -- the archival record (ondertrouw 9 September 1712, Amsterdam Stadsarchief; marriage 25 September 1712, Haarlem) contradicts April; genealogieonline independently gives permission 9 September 1712 and marriage about 25 September 1712 (leg 1 F4; see C549). The couple had one son and three daughters: Etienne (15 May 1713 - 30 May 1713), Anna (1714-?), Catharina (1716-1795), Angelique Hester (1717-1792). EN Wikipedia instead says "In May 1713 the couple had male twins, who both died within a few weeks; he portrayed them both" -- the specialist site documents a single son, born 15 May, buried 30 May, with a red-chalk portrait inscribed "Etiene Picart mort a trois jours" (leg 1 F4; see C549).

On 4 November 1713 Picart was registered as a burgher (poorter) of Amsterdam with the occupation plate cutter (plaatsnijder); three days later, on 27 November, he entered the booksellers', printers' and bookbinders' guild book as an art dealer (konstverkoper). "He was a serious hard worker and earned enough with his own print shop to provide his family with a well to do life. There is no indication that Picart dealed/traded in anything other than in prints, and in books illustrated by himself, most of them of a very high quality". With five friends he came together every Monday evening to talk about art -- "On occasion, they got so excited that sometimes they even forgot to empty the bottle of wine". His three daughters were baptized in the Westerkerk.

From the moment he arrived in the Netherlands "the orders flocked, mostly from Huguenot book publishers, but also from private clients. They felt attracted to the elegant classicism of Picart's inventions and to the finish of his prints". NL Wikipedia adds a harder edge: "De veelheid aan opdrachten ging ten koste aan de kwaliteit, niettemin verdiende hij veel geld" -- the multitude of commissions came at the cost of quality, yet he earned a great deal of money. FR Wikipedia, drawing on the older French biographical tradition (Michaud, Portalis & Beraldi), makes the same charge at length: the booksellers pressed him, "On exige de lui des ouvrages froids et leches... Il gagne beaucoup d'argent, mais aux depens de sa reputation; de son vivant meme, il voit les connaisseurs ne faire cas que de ses premiers ouvrages". This tension -- volume against finish, market against connoisseurship -- runs through his whole Amsterdam career and is one of the study's central facts, not a footnote.

The work itself, in the order the sources give it:

- **1715-1729: the twelve Epithalames.** Twelve wedding prints, all his own invention except the last (by Louis Fabricius Dubourg), each with a Latin motto, made to adorn booklets of poetry published for a wedding or wedding anniversary. The first, Nova gaudia surgunt ("New joy rises"), marked the silver wedding anniversary of the Mennonites Maria van der Palm and Jan van Vollenhoven on 16 July 1715. They were mainly commissioned by a small group of wealthy Mennonites in Amsterdam and Haarlem -- Van Lennep, Van Vollenhoven, De Neufville -- who had made fortunes in overseas trade and now acted as bankers and insurers. After enough prints were pulled for family and friends, the copperplate was gilded and put in a frame for the married couple. The prints are small -- mostly 106 x 143 mm to 252 x 203 mm, etching and engraving, signed "B. Picart invenit et sculpsit" with the year. After 1729 Picart published a brochure for collectors, Explication de diverses planches inventees et gravees pour des Epithalames ou noces par Bernard Picart, explaining the scenes and translating the Latin mottos. A thirteenth Epithalame was invented but never etched and engraved in his lifetime. See mechanisms M6, M7, M10.
- **1718:** frontispiece, with Cornelis de Bruyn, for Reizen over Moskovie, door Persie en Indie.
- **1720:** the Robinson Crusoe portrait frontispiece for L'Honore & Chatelain's French translation, signed "B. Picart. del. sculp. direxit 1720" -- redacted at the publishers' request for the Dutch translation, which used a second plate without the credit line; the design was then copied by Frankfurt, Leipzig and Nuremberg publishers and in Italy. Also 1720: Figures de la Bible, and the anonymous folio Het Groote Tafereel der Dwaasheid (The Great Mirror of Folly), published within months of the 1720 stock-market crashes, "without an author or a publisher listed, although many now connect the volume largely to Picart". One plate, engraved by Bernard Baron after Picart's design, shows a street scene with a cart of Fortuna pulled by six allegorical figures for investment schemes, a devil blowing soap bubbles, and three background buildings inscribed "T'Ziekenhuis" (Hospital), "T'Gekkenhuis" (Asylum) and "Arm-Huis" (Poor House).
- **From 1720: the Ceremonies.** Picart collaborated from 1720 with Jean-Frederic Bernard (1683-1744), "the son of a Protestant minister... with a commitment to religious toleration", on Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses de tous les peuples du monde. Bernard announced the series in the Journal des Scavans in January 1721, marketing it with a newly invented letter font, the most beautiful paper, and sample pages exhibited at his shop in the Kalverstraat. The first volumes appeared in 1723. The original French edition ran to seven folio volumes, 1723-1737, with two further volumes added by Bernard thereafter; EN Wikipedia counts "ten volumes of text and 266 engravings"; GAMEO says seven volumes "with supplements eventually reaching 12 volumes"; FR Wikipedia says 11 volumes in-folio, 1723-43. Plate counts disagree across sources: 243, "more than 250", 263, 266 (leg 1 F8; see C543). Bernard supplied the ~3,000 pages of text -- "Though uncredited, J-F. Bernard wrote most of the scrupulously footnoted text" -- while "All the engravings were designed by Picart but he was assisted by his seven students and several other accomplished engravers". The work sold a remarkable 3,000 copies, was translated into Dutch (1727-38, trans. Abraham Moubach), English (from 1731/1733) and German (by 1746), was banned by the Catholic Church in Rome in 1738, and was reissued in bowdlerized form in Paris in 1741 by the abbes Banier and Le Mascrier, retaining all the original Picart engravings. Its images "became the standard means of portraying many of the world's religions until well into the nineteenth century". Jonathan I. Israel calls it "an immense effort to record the religious rituals and beliefs of the world in all their diversity as objectively and authentically as possible" (see Q9). The first volume put Judaism first, immediately after the introduction, drawing on Leon Modena's Riti Ebraica and Basnage's Histoire des Juifs; Picart gained access to the Portuguese Synagogue and attended a Passover Seder at the home of the prominent Curiel (d'Acosta) family.
- **1724:** with the Prussian antiquarian Philipp von Stosch, Gemmae Antiquae Caelatae (Pierres antiques gravees) -- Picart's engravings reproduced 70 antique carved hardstones (onyx, jasper, carnelian) from European collections, "a volume of inestimable value to antiquarians and historians"; published Amsterdam: Bernard Picart, 1724, with 70 richly engraved plates of enlarged gemstone designs, Latin and French titles in red and black.
- **1728:** Les Cesars de l'empereur Julien, "enrichies de plus de 300 medailles... graves par Bernard Picart le Romain".
- **1729:** Recueil de "Lions", dessines d'apres nature par divers maitres et graves par Bernard Picart -- 42 etchings after Durer, Le Brun and Rembrandt.
- **1731:** a reprint originally by his father.
- **1733:** Le Temple des Muses -- an illustrated book of Ovid's more popular fables, published in Dutch in 1733 (Tempel der zanggodinnen...), in English in 1738, in French in 1742, by Zacharias Chatelain; 60 plates (LX), captions in French, English, German and Dutch; text by Antoine de La Barre de Beaumarchais. The plates are "all finely engraved and are composed with a central part dedicated to the illustration of the story and a larger one, framing the previous, inhabited with details inspired to the central plate". FR Wikipedia lists the artists involved as Michel de Marolles, Bernard Picart, Jacques Favereau, Abraham van Diepenbeeck and Cornelis Bloemaert -- these are the designers of the source compositions, a 17th-century set, not co-engravers; the STCN record says "Engraved by B. Picart le Romain et al" (leg 1 F15).

### The school

"At some time Picart opened an engraving school. His pupils included Jacob Folkema, Jakob van der Schley (who portrayed him posthumously), Pieter Tanje and Francois Morellon la Cave, who all used his drawings for engravings. According to RKD, Johann Philipp Endelich (?-1760) was also a pupil". In 1723/1726 his mother-in-law Anna Yver and two of her children lived at Rokin; "Picart may have used most floors for teaching drawing and engraving or storing paper". FR Wikipedia's pupil list is much longer and includes French names -- Joseph Aved, Claude Dubosc, Louis Fabricius Dubourg, Edme Jeaurat, Louis Surugue, Henri Simon Thomassin, alongside Folkema and Van der Schley -- which likely mixes Paris-period pupils with Amsterdam ones; the two lists are not reconciled in any source read (leg 1 F9). The "seven students" who assisted on the Ceremonies are not named as a set in any source read. The school is the hinge of the chain: this is the Amsterdam engraving line that Reinier Vinkeles (#543) inherited a generation later -- Picart's pupils' pupils. (That descent itself is asserted by the chain, not independently verified in this leg; see leg 1 F26 and C548.)

### Death and the widow's settlement, 1732-1738

The Eloge reports Picart felt ill on 8 November 1732; "Despite the beneficial effect of donkey milk Picart died May 8, 1733, almost sixty years old. May 13 he was buried in the Walloon church". His memorial portrait -- etched and engraved by Jacob van der Schley, "the most important pupil of Picart", after a pastel by Matthaeus des Angles painted about a month before he fell ill -- shows him in an oval frame partly covered by heavy drapery, with allegorical figures below: History holding a closed book, the Genius of Drawing in grief, the Genius of Engraving offering the crown of immortality. The British Museum's impression (1901,1022.709) is lettered "Bernard Picart / le Romain / Dessinateur et Graveur, / Ne a Paris le 11 Juin 1673. Mort a Amsterdam le 8 Mai 1733" and "Jacobus van der Schley inv. & sculp. 1734" (snippet-sourced; the BM page itself failed to render -- leg 1 F19).

Anna Picart continued the business, "which was not unusual in that time". On 3 May 1734 she made a testament naming executors and advisers on "the art and the business exercised by her husband", Jean de Bary and Isaac Walraven. In 1734 she published Impostures innocentes, ou recueil d'estampes d'apres divers peintres illustres tels que Raphael, Le Guide, Carlo Maratta, Le Poussin, Rembrandt, etc., gravees a leur imitation et selon le gout particulier de chacun d'eux, et accompagnees d'un discours sur les prejuges de certains curieux touchant la gravure, par Bernard Picart, dessinateur et graveur, avec son eloge historique et le catalogue de ses ouvrages (Veuve de Bernard Picart, Amsterdam) -- the Discours written by Picart in 1732, the Eloge historique written by Anna Vincent herself, "undoubtedly with the help of the bibliographer Prosper Marchand". She also published Pierre Lebrun's Superstitions anciennes et modernes with Picart's engravings (J.-F. Bernard, 1733-1736). She ordered her three daughters to keep his collection of drawings together but sell the prints at auction and the copperplates in Paris. Anna died on 5 March 1736 and was buried first class in the Nieuwe Kerk; EN Wikipedia gives her dates as 1684-1736, while the specialist site's header writes "(1684-1737?)" (leg 1 F5/F10). Her estate inventory was made up on 17 April 1736 (specialist site, citing Stadsarchief Amsterdam Notaris Ardinois 9126, 347), not 12 March 1736 as EN Wikipedia states (leg 1 F11; see C544). It listed about 400 portfolios with copperplates, books, drawings, paper, 54 paintings (not specified), jewellery and bonds; the specialist site's footnote quotes the inventory directly: "Door het geheele huijs zo in de kamers als op de solder zijn bevonden vier en vijftig stuks schilderijen". Each daughter received 10,844.70 guilders. The paintings were sold publicly on 5 May 1737 despite the testament's instruction to sell them privately; the drawings that should have been divided among the children were also auctioned (Amsterdam, 25 November 1737); and in June 1738 "une belle partie de planches de cuivre gravees la pluspart par Bernard Picart" went under the hammer in Paris. "The death of Anna Picart forever ended a flourishing print trade".

The Rijksmuseum website "has more than 2,000 works online by Bernard Picart" (EN Wikipedia) -- Wikipedia's figure, not verified live in this leg (leg 1 F24; see C545). His total printed oeuvre is put at about 1,300 prints (McClintock & Strong, 1870, snippet-sourced; FR Wikipedia: the Catalogue general in Impostures innocentes "compose de plus de 1 300 planches"). Both trace to the same 1734 catalogue; treat as a catalogue count, not a modern census. The Rijksmuseum "2,000+ works online" counts impressions, states and related objects, not distinct compositions (leg 1 F16).

## M1. Dessine d'apres nature: drawing from life as a truth-claim

Picart went into the Portuguese Synagogue and into Jewish homes to draw ceremonies on the spot, and signed the claim on the plate itself: the circumcision-instruments plate is signed "Dessine d'apres nature par B. Picart 1725". He attended a Passover Seder at the Curiel (d'Acosta) family home so he could label his depiction "dessine d'apres nature". The Lions of 1729 are likewise "dessines d'apres nature par divers maitres". The mechanism: the signature "d'apres nature" is not decoration, it is the plate's evidence -- it tells the reader the image was witnessed, not copied from a travelogue. Hunt, Jacob and Mijnhardt note he drew from real-life models "whenever possible", and when he could not gain personal access he drew from liturgical booklets, witness accounts or travelogues. The watchable behaviour: a ceremony rendered as a specific room, with specific people in contemporary dress, rather than as a timeless biblical tableau -- compare his circumcision scene with Leusden's 1657 woodcut, where Jews wear stereotypical priestly vestments. **Takeaway:** the claim "I was there" is engraved into the plate; for a generative piece, the signature of witnessing -- a named room, a dated sitting -- is itself the device.

## M2. The explanatory key beneath the plate

The circumcision engraving carries "a key beneath the engraving [that] identifies important characters in the foreground and background, along with their respective roles" -- the father kneeling, the godfather holding the boy, the mohel at centre stage, a figure with a chalice, and an "NB." explaining that the women visible are Christian because Jewish women do not attend the ceremony. The synagogue-dedication plate likewise uses "the encyclopedic feature of an explanatory key at the bottom of the page". The mechanism: the image is a diagram with a legend; the viewer reads the picture by reading the key, and the key is what makes a strange rite legible to a stranger. It is the print equivalent of a map legend. **Takeaway:** a key turns a crowd scene into a legible system -- every figure gets a role, and the roles are the picture.

## M3. Con-visualisation: the ceremony plate plus the tool plate

Von Wyss-Giacosa's term, taken up by Nellis: Picart pairs two representational modes so they explain each other. The circumcision ceremony (wide, populated, theatrical) is paired with Instrumens qui servent a la Circoncision (1725), a close-up of the mohel's tools -- "a ceremonial circumcision knife, a silver shield that facilitated periah..., bandages, and a flask of astringent". "By providing his spectators with both a generic close-up of the tools used during a circumcision and a representation of the proceedings of such a ceremony, Picart manages to make tangible a for a Western European audience very abstract, and hitherto little documented liturgical practice". The reader is expected to flip back and forth: "One can imagine the ways in which early modern readers might have taken the book in hand when they came across these pages, zooming in and out on the engravings themselves and flipping back and forth between the various sheets". The mechanism is a deliberate two-plate rhythm: wide shot, then macro. **Takeaway:** show the ritual, then show the instruments -- the pair teaches what neither plate alone can.

## M4. Collage: assembling one engraving from many circulating sources

"Taking up his spectators into a flurry of images that were circulating throughout and beyond the Dutch Republic and relying on a collage technique to assemble different details within one and the same engraving, Picart found a way to alter dominant and biased views on various religious ceremonies". For the circumcision he worked from and against earlier depictions -- Leusden (1657), Romeyn de Hooghe (1665), Jan Luyken (1683) -- adopting Luyken's overall composition and main characters while changing the dress, the separation of men and women, and the inclusion of Christian women as assistants. The mechanism: the plate is a composite, not a copy; borrowed scaffolding carries corrected detail. **Takeaway:** collage is Picart's editing tool -- keep the familiar composition, swap in the observed fact.

## M5. Accumulative theatricality: the ceremony as a staged scene

Nellis's central argument: Picart imports theatrical techniques into the extra-theatrical medium of print -- "representing the spectators themselves, by making the mise en scene, the techniques deployed to theatricalize the event, visible, and by addressing the onlooker himself as a spectator". In the Ceremonies this works two ways: accumulative theatricality (importing theatrical techniques, underscoring the theatrical nature of the ceremonies) and an immersive approach (engravings of specific ceremony details that draw the spectator further in). His synagogue dedication deliberately drops de Hooghe's simultaneous viewpoints, floor plan, street view and allegorical superstructure in favour of "a snapshot of one specific moment during the inauguration" -- while keeping the procession of Torah scrolls as dramatic temporal activity. A related framing device appears in Le Temple des Muses: each plate is "composed with a central part dedicated to the illustration of the story and a larger one, framing the previous, inhabited with details inspired to the central plate" -- the frame is a second stage around the story. **Takeaway:** a ceremony is not illustrated, it is staged -- the viewer is seated in the audience and shown the stage machinery too.

## M6. The small piece: vignette, cul-de-lampe, epithalame

The Eloge praise, quoted on the specialist site, is precise about scale: Picart "excelled in the beautiful invention & the beautiful order of the subjects that he had to deal with, the engraving of small pieces, like his epithalames, his vignettes, his cul-de-lampe/tail pieces". The twelve Epithalames are mostly 106 x 143 mm to 252 x 203 mm; the Eloge calls them "des pieces extremement delicates & finies". FR Wikipedia's critical reception quotes the widow's Eloge again: he "ne faisait presque rien que d'apres nature... il portait toujours des tablettes sur lui, ainsi il tirait parti de tout: la mer, la campagne, le paysage, les plantes, la ville, la foule, les foires, les voitures publiques". The mechanism: smallness is not a limitation but a finish standard -- the vignette is where the burin is most delicate, and the small format is what the collectors (curieux) wanted to own. **Takeaway:** the masterpiece can be 12 cm wide; delicacy of finish is the ambition, not scale.

## M7. The title print as the soul of the book

Gerard Lairesse's Groot Schilderboek (1707; English 1738/1778) is the only contemporary textual source on title prints, and Bartelings uses it to read Picart: a title print should (1) delight the eye, (2) tend to the praise and honour of the author and designer, (3) be advantageous to the seller. Lairesse's image: "the book is also compared with a body, and the print with the soul that animates this body"; the image should be displayed "in a 'theatrical stage opened on one or both sides with a curtain, sometimes setting it off with a colonaded frontispiece, or else inclosing it in a moulding or compartment'", with light falling from the left side of the opening of the book. Book prints differ from fine prints: a book print "express the matter which is represented, even were if designed in white marble, bas relief, nay, in snow or sand", while a fine print considers only the master and his art -- "For this reason, book prints stand in need of an explanation, but other prints not: for the colour is in the one, what the writing is in the other". The Epithalames enact this: image and title-print poem form a "synergetic relation" -- the print shows the allegory, the poem names the persons and explains the attributes; "Together, image and text are tailored to the specific wedding couple and as such form a synergetic relation". **Takeaway:** the frontispiece is not a cover, it is the book's soul -- it must delight, honour, sell, and be explained.

## M8. Imitation of manners: the Impostures innocentes

Picart's posthumous collection (Amsterdam, 1734, published by his widow) gathers engravings "d'apres divers peintres illustres tels que Raphael, Le Guide, Carlo Maratta, Le Poussin, Rembrandt, etc., gravees a leur imitation et selon le gout particulier de chacun d'eux" -- each plate engraved in the particular manner of its model. FR Wikipedia: "Il a, en outre, un talent particulier pour imiter la maniere de divers maitres; et les pieces qu'il a executees dans le style de Rembrandt, du Guide, etc., ont trompe plus d'un connaisseur. Il appelait ces morceaux des 'impostures innocentes'". The Met's copy (object 45.131, Harris Brisbane Dick Fund 1945) lists designers Raphael, Guido Reni, Nicolas Poussin, Carlo Maratti and Rembrandt for a single volume. The collection is also a treatise: it carries Picart's Discours sur les prejuges de certains curieux touchant la gravure (written 1732), a defence of reproductive engraving, plus the Eloge historique and the catalogue of his works. Ann Jensen Adams, quoted in FR Wikipedia: "Picart stands at the cusp of a change in attitude toward the reproductive print... Picart's etchings argue for the status (and market value) of reproductive engravings in general and of those produced by their author in particular, treading a fine line between reproduction and authenticity. In the process of creating faithful reproductions of other artists, he creates authentic Picarts" (see Q10). **Takeaway:** imitation so exact it fools the connoisseur is not forgery but authorship -- the copy, perfectly made, becomes an original.

## M9. Burin and point combined

FR Wikipedia's biography: "Aussi habile a manier le burin que la pointe, il execute une foule de petits sujets, dans lesquels il combine avec adresse les deux procedes, et ou l'on admirait une fecondite d'invention et une habilete de main qui le firent comparer a Sebastien Leclerc". The Epithalames are catalogued as "etching and engraving" throughout, and signed "invenit et sculpsit" -- he both designed and cut. Lairesse's dictum, quoted by Bartelings: "the art of engraving... respects painting, as painting does nature... Painting consists in a neat and good outline, proportion, light and shade: and these are also the foundations of engraving" -- and "By colour Lairesse means suggesting colour by varying the distance between the etching and the engraving lines". The mechanism: etching for the loose, drawing-like line, burin for the finished, controlled line; colour suggested by line-spacing, not by ink. **Takeaway:** two tools, one hand -- the etched line sketches, the burin finishes, and their spacing stands in for colour.

## M10. The gilded copperplate as a wedding object

After the Epithalame run was printed for family and friends, "the copperplate was gilded and put in a frame for the married couple". The Eloge says the same: "Des qu'on en a tire le nombre convenable pour les parens & amis, on dore ordinairement la planche qu'on met en bordure". Bartelings notes she has "not yet tracked down such a gilded copperplate". The mechanism: the printing plate itself, the tool of reproduction, is retired from use, gilded, and reframed as a unique domestic object -- the edition ends by turning its own matrix into a keepsake. The print's value is anchored in the plate's transformation. **Takeaway:** the plate is not just a means to prints; gilded and framed, it becomes the wedding present itself.

## M11. Self-printing as quality control

Picart "was dissatisfied with the printing quality of some publishers, and according to the Eloge historique, he had stipulated that he could print copies of all his work for himself before the delivery of the copper plates to his clients... Picart really watched over the quality of the impressions. Picart printed these images on better quality paper with wider margins and sold them to print collectors (curieux)". The Eloge (quoted by Bartelings): he resolved "de n'entreprendre aucun Ouvrage, qu'il ne lui fut libre d'en tirer 100. ou du moins 50. Epreuves, a ses frais: & comme il faisoit imprimer cela dans sa maison, sous ses yeux, & avec un grand soin, ce sont toutes Epreuves magnifiques, & sur de tres beau papier choisi. (...) la pluspart des Libraires la faisoient tirer, ou plutot massacre, a si vil prix & par de si mauvais imprimeurs, qu'a peine reconnoissoit-il lui-meme son propre Ouvrage, & qu'il ne le prenoit a la premiere vue que pour une mauvaise Copie". He was "rather unhappy with the impressions of his Massacre des innocents, and the title print in a translation of Plinius' 35th book". The mechanism: retain the right to the first 50-100 proofs, print them yourself, on chosen paper, under your own eyes -- the artist's proof as a contractual right, not a courtesy. **Takeaway:** the print is only finished when the artist has pulled it himself; the publisher's impression is a different, lesser object.

## M12. The school as a division of labour: design by Picart, execution by the shop

"All the engravings were designed by Picart but he was assisted by his seven students and several other accomplished engravers". His pupils "all used his drawings for engravings". The division is visible in the signatures and in the record: the Ceremonies Mennonite baptism was "engraved by Balthasar Bernaert in 1732" after Picart; the Great Mirror of Folly plate was "Engraved by Bernard Baron... after a design by Bernard Picart"; Quirijn Fonbonne engraved after Picart's drawing of the Affliction of the Israelites. The Epithalames invert the pattern: there Picart signs "invenit et sculpsit" -- he kept the small, delicate, high-value pieces for his own hand, and let the shop execute the large survey work from his designs. The Rokin house -- mother-in-law and children on some floors, "most floors for teaching drawing and engraving or storing paper" -- is the physical plant of this system. **Takeaway:** the atelier is a machine with two gears -- Picart invents, the school executes, and he reserves his own burin for the pieces that carry his name most closely.

## Works

## W1. La Felicite de la Regence (The Happiness of the Regency), 1704

Date 1704 (signed and dated "B. P. 1704" on the plate). Medium engraving on paper. Dimensions height 49.5 cm (cut) x width 34.6 cm (cut) -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 68 lines). Current location Victoria and Albert Museum, London, accession E.3202-1960; Prints, Drawings & Paintings Collection; given by Edgar Seligman. Not on display (no image online). What it shows mechanically: Marie de Medicis seated on a throne, holding a scale of justice in one hand and a sceptre in the other, surrounded by allegorical and mythological figures. A reproductive engraving after Peter Paul Rubens, from a drawing by Jean-Marc Nattier (lettering: "Rubens pinxit J. M. Nattier delineavit B. Picart Sculpsit. A Paris chez le Sr. Nattier... Avec Privilege du Roy"). One of a three-print series, "Galerie du Luxembourg", reproducing Rubens's Marie de Medicis cycle then in the Palais du Luxembourg. Mechanically it is a large Paris-period reproductive plate: a single enthroned figure anchors a symmetrical crowd of personifications, the burin work carrying the modelling that in Rubens was paint -- a translation of colour and flesh into graded hatching. The plate is cut (trimmed), so the recorded size is a surviving fragment, not necessarily the issued sheet.

## W2. The Affliction of the Israelites (L'Affliction des Israelites), 1712

Date 1712 (signed and dated "B. Picart f 1712" lower left). Medium pen and grey ink, grey wash, applied stylus (a drawing, not a print). Dimensions 32.5 x 21.5 cm (12.8 x 8.46 in.) -- reported-but-unverified (auction/dealer snippet; not on a museum or auction page read end to end). Current location private collection (Ile-de-France, at last sale listing); provenance includes Baron Frederik Carel Theodoor van Isendoorn van de Cannenborch (1784-1865, collector's mark L.1407 verso). An engraved version after it by Quirijn Fonbonne is in the Rijksmuseum (RP-P-1934-252, per the same snippet). What it shows mechanically: a biblical lamentation scene -- the Israelites in affliction -- drawn in grey ink with grey wash, the stylus used to incise/transfer lines. It belongs to the picture-Bible project later published by G. Hoet in Amsterdam and by Picart in 1720 (Figures de la Bible). Mechanically it is a design drawing: wash establishes tonal masses that the engraver (Fonbonne) then had to re-encode as burin hatching, so the drawing is the tonal script and the print is its linear translation. The verso annotation "Rouw der Israelrahen le Giben" shows the drawing circulated in Dutch hands.

## W3. The Massacre (Slaughter) of the Innocents, 1715

Date 1715. Medium engraving (per Philadelphia Museum of Art record). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found in any source read or searched. Current location Philadelphia Museum of Art (PMA record, search snippets). No accession number recovered. What it shows mechanically: Herod's soldiers slaughtering the infants while mothers try to protect them -- a crowded massacre scene after a design of Picart's own. The 19th-century Cyclopedia of Biblical, Theological and Ecclesiastical Literature calls it "one of the best of them... after a design of his own: there are various impressions of it". Mechanically it is an original composition rather than a reproductive copy: Picart stages the violence as interlocking bodies in the foreground, the horror carried by the press of figures rather than by a single focal group -- the compositional habit (a dense foreground frieze) that recurs in his later ceremonial crowd plates. Picart was "rather unhappy with the impressions" of this plate (Eloge via Bartelings) -- the work whose printing drove him to the self-printing stipulation of M11.

## W4. Robinson Crusoe (portrait frontispiece), 1720

Date 1720 (plate signed "B. Picart. del. sculp. direxit 1720"). Medium copper engraving, frontispiece to La Vie et les Avantures Surprenantes de Robinson Crusoe (Amsterdam: L'Honore & Chatelain, 1720), French translation of Defoe. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found. Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (Artera mirror lists a "Robinson Crusoe", 1720, Rijksmuseum; not directly verified). Copies also in the Beinecke Library, Yale. What it shows mechanically: a standing portrait of Crusoe on his island in animal-skin garments, with muskets and sword -- the visual identity of Crusoe that then propagated across Europe. Per the Cambridge Element (snippet): L'Honore & Chatelain instructed Picart to undertake the portrait frontispiece; at least one further plate of the same design exists without the Picart credit line, used for subsequent editions; the Dutch translation (de Jansoons van Waesberge, 1720) redacted the plate, keeping only Picart's figure design and revising the setting. Mechanically it is a portrait-as-emblem: one figure, fully equipped, standing for the whole novel -- costume as character, with the island reduced to a backdrop. The design was copied by Frankfurt, Leipzig and Nuremberg publishers and in Italy. The signature "del. sculp. direxit" -- designed, engraved, directed -- is the first appearance of the director's credit that marks the Amsterdam period.

## W5. La Republique de Hollande sur son Trone (The Republic of Holland on her Throne), 1722

Date 1722 (lettered "B. Picart invenit et fecit 1722"). Medium engraving on paper. Dimensions height 33 cm x width 21.9 cm -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 61 lines). Current location Victoria and Albert Museum, London, accession E.3203-1960; given by Edgar Seligman. Not on display (no image online). What it shows mechanically: an allegory of the Republic of Holland enthroned, lettered with six lines of French, after Picart's own design ("invenit et fecit" -- he both invented and executed it). Mechanically it is a political emblem-plate: a single enthroned personification carrying the whole meaning, with the explanatory text doing the work that a crowd scene would do in the Ceremonies plates. The small upright format (33 x 21.9 cm) is the book-illustration scale Picart settles into in Amsterdam, against the large 1704 Rubens plate (W1).

## W6. The Circumcision of the Portuguese Jews and the Redemption of the Firstborn (La Circoncision des Juifs Portugais), 1722

Date 1722 (see flag: a 2024 Ghent article captions the plate 1722 but calls it a "1725 engraving" in its text; Meisterdrucke dates it 1725). Medium engraving. Dimensions 33.4 x 22 cm -- reported-but-unverified (Ghent University article, search snippet; Rijksmuseum object cited as hdl.handle.net/10934/RM0001.COLLECT.308497; Rijksmuseum page not opened directly). Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (per the Ghent article). Wikipedia states an impression is "in the collection of the Jewish Museum of Switzerland" -- a different impression of the same plate. What it shows mechanically: the moment just before the circumcision of a newborn in the Portuguese-Jewish community of Amsterdam, paired with the redemption of the firstborn. Picart drew on direct observation of Amsterdam's Jews (his sketches are said to be in the Stedelijk Museum, Amsterdam, per Encyclopedia.com). Mechanically it is documentary staging: a domestic ritual interior rendered as a legible ceremony -- figures arranged so each participant's role and gesture can be read, the rite itself treated as a sequence of visible actions rather than as a symbol. This is the plate that makes the Ceremonies' method visible: observed ritual, engraved as evidence. See M1, M2, M4.

## W7. Bacchus and Ariadne (magnified gemstone), c. 1722

Date c. 1722; plate XXII of Gemmae Antiquae Caelatae. Medium etching and copperplate engraving on laid paper. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found. Current location not established (dealer listing, Vinterior, search snippet; no museum holding recovered). What it shows mechanically: an antique engraved gem -- Bacchus and Ariadne -- reproduced greatly enlarged from the original hardstone. The Gemmae plates reproduce 70 antique carved hardstones (onyx, jasper, carnelian) from European collections at magnified scale so the carving can be studied. Mechanically the plate is a facsimile exercise: the burin imitates the gem-cutter's shallow incisions, translating a tiny three-dimensional intaglio into a large flat print in which light and shadow stand in for the stone's depth. The point is fidelity, not invention -- the opposite of the Impostures manner (see W20 and Evolution).

## W8. Diana Montana / Apollonii Opus, 1723

Date 1723. Medium engraving and etching on paper. Dimensions height 268 mm x width 187 mm -- reported-but-unverified (British Museum snippet; the BM object page itself returned only boilerplate and was NOT counted as read). Current location British Museum, London, registration number 1872,1012.1658; Prints and Drawings; not on display. What it shows mechanically: portrait of Diana standing full-length in profile to the right, holding a torch, bow and arrows on her shoulders, set in an oval frame within a rectangle, with an inscription in ancient Greek. Illustration to Gemmae antiquae caelatae / Pierres antiques gravees (Amsterdam: Bernard Picart, 1724). Mechanically it is the gem-catalogue formula at its most disciplined: the figure isolated in an oval cartouche, the stone's shape dictating the composition, Greek lettering reproduced as part of the object -- the print as a museum label that is also the specimen.

## W9. Caput Ignutum / Agathopi Opus, 1723

Date 1723. Medium engraving and etching on paper. Dimensions height 270 mm x width 187 mm -- reported-but-unverified (British Museum snippet; BM object page not counted -- see above). Current location British Museum, London, registration number 1872,1012.1680; Prints and Drawings; not on display. What it shows mechanically: head of an unidentified sitter in profile to the right, in an oval frame within a rectangle, with an inscription in ancient Greek; illustration to Gemmae antiquae caelatae (Amsterdam: Bernard Picart, 1724). Same formula as W8 -- the pair shows the catalogue's serial discipline: same sheet size (within 2 mm), same oval-in-rectangle armature, the only variable being the stone itself. Mechanically the series works by repetition: the frame does not change, so the viewer's eye compares gems, not compositions.

## W10. Performances of Religious Ceremonies in the Incas, 1723

Date 1723. Medium etching / engraving on paper (Amsterdam). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found. Current location not established (stock-photo listing, SuperStock, search snippet; no museum holding recovered). Attribution in that listing is "Bernard Picart (workshop or), After Bernard Picart" -- see flag. What it shows mechanically: a single leaf with two stacked performances. Above: Peruvians dance and make music during a lunar eclipse. Below: a prisoner bound to a pole is sacrificed; Peruvians cut his body. A caption in French runs among the performances. From Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses. Mechanically it is the two-tier plate: two separate scenes share one sheet and one caption system, so the reader compares a communal rite (above) with a violent one (below) as two entries in the same comparative catalogue. The stacking is the argument -- religions laid out side by side, and rites stacked within a single religion.

## W11. King of Tartars; Trophies, 1723-1737

Date 1723-1737 (museum date range for the Ceremonies run). Medium etching. Dimensions image 5 11/16 x 8 1/8 in. (14.45 x 20.64 cm); plate 6 1/2 x 8 3/4 in. (16.51 x 22.23 cm); sheet 6 11/16 x 9 5/16 in. (16.99 x 23.65 cm) -- VERIFIED (Nelson-Atkins page read end to end, 29 lines). Current location The Nelson-Atkins Museum of Art, Kansas City (object report). No accession number given on the page read. What it shows mechanically: two images on one plate: the King of the Tartars and a trophy grouping. A small-format Ceremonies plate, the image barely larger than a postcard (14.45 x 20.64 cm). Mechanically it shows the economy of the Ceremonies system at its smallest: two subjects share a plate to save copper and paper, the plate mark (16.51 x 22.23 cm) and sheet (16.99 x 23.65 cm) nearly coincident -- the print is trimmed close, made to be bound and read, not framed.

## W12. L'Incas consacre son Vaze au Soleil / L'Incas vient recevoir les Ofrandes que ses Sujets font au Soleil (The Inca consecrates his vase to the Sun / receives his subjects' offerings), c. 1723

Date c. 1723 (dealer listing; French edition of Ceremonies given as 1721-1725 in the same listing -- see flag). Medium engraving (from Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses, French edition). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found. Current location not established (antique-map dealer listing, search snippet). What it shows mechanically: two scenes of the Incas in South America on one sheet. Upper view: the Inca consecrates his vase to the sun. Lower view: the Inca comes to receive the offerings his subjects make to the sun. Same two-tier stacking as W10: the sheet is a diptych of a single ritual logic (offering upward, tribute inward), the sun as the absent centre that both scenes face. The European mediation is total -- Picart never left Europe and worked from travel accounts -- so the "Inca" architecture and costume are European stage scenery dressed around an American rite. Contrast W6: the same even-handed documentary finish applied to a rite he witnessed and to a rite he only read about -- the evenness is the Enlightenment argument, and its danger (see Evolution).

## W13. Qui suis je? ou suis je? et d'ou suis je venu? (Who am I? Where am I? and why am I here? / Adam awakening into the new Creation), 1720s-30s

Date 1720s-30s (V&A dating; undated within that range). Medium engraving. Dimensions height 5.375 in. x width 3.125 in. (13.65 x 7.94 cm) -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 51 lines). Contained in one of three bound volumes measuring 17 x 13 in., holding 364 engravings. Current location Victoria and Albert Museum, London, accession E.173-1941; bequeathed by Eustace F. Bosanquet. An image is online (unlike W1/W5). What it shows mechanically: Adam awakening into the new Creation -- a scene from the story of Adam and Eve, titled with three existential questions. One of 364 prints in a set of three bound volumes (E.12 to 375-1941), i.e. a Bible-illustration plate. Mechanically it is the smallest documented Picart here (13.65 x 7.94 cm): a pocket-sized engraving in which a vast subject (the first consciousness) is compressed into a vignette, the questions in the title doing the philosophical work the tiny image cannot. The title-as-caption is the mechanism: the words frame the awakening as inquiry, not just narrative.

## W14. Voorwerpen die bij een joodse besnijdenis dienst doen / De boekrollen van de Thora (Objects used at a Jewish circumcision / The scrolls of the Torah), 1725

Date 1725. Medium engraving (per Google Arts & Culture record). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- the GAC record gives only "papier" (paper) as physical dimensions. Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (via Google Arts & Culture, search snippet; Rijksmuseum page not opened directly). What it shows mechanically: a still-life catalogue plate: the implements used at a Jewish circumcision laid out as objects, paired with the scrolls of the Torah. Companion to W6 -- where W6 stages the rite with people, W14 inventories its tools. Mechanically it is the specimen-plate mode (same habit as the gems in W8/W9): objects isolated, evenly lit by hatching, arranged for identification rather than drama. The Ceremonies' comparative method here becomes literal taxonomy: first the ceremony, then the kit. See M3.

## W15. Pompe funebre des rois de Tunquin (Funeral Procession of the Kings of Tonkin), 1729

Date 1729 (inscribed lower left "B. Picart sculp. dir. 1729"). Medium etching (dealer page; the Rijksmuseum impression is catalogued as an etching/engraving from Ceremonies). Dimensions image 13 1/2 x 16 3/4 in.; sheet 16 1/8 x 19 1/2 in. -- reported-but-unverified (1stDibs dealer page read end to end, 89 lines -- dealer page, so not VERIFIED). The same page states the Rijksmuseum records 343 x 425 mm for its impression -- also reported-but-unverified here, since the Rijksmuseum page was not opened. Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam, accession RP-P-1911-3172 (gift of M. Onnes van Nijenrode, 1911), per the dealer page. The dealer's impression is a separate detached impression. What it shows mechanically: the royal funeral ceremonies of Tonkin (northern Vietnam): across a broad landscape a procession winds through successive horizontal registers and doubles back across the terrain, culminating in a flotilla of ceremonial vessels. A French key identifies officials bearing maces, a monument with the deceased king's name, elephants, horses, a funerary carriage drawn by eight deer, the new king and his brothers, princesses carrying food for the deceased, princes with musicians, provincial governors, wagons of gold and silk; the boats carry the royal body and burial treasures. Mechanically it is the panoramic inventory: the procession's doubling-back lets a huge cast stay legible, dark foreground against lighter distant hills gives depth, and the lettered key turns the landscape into a captioned diagram. The source was Jean-Baptiste Tavernier's account of Tonkin -- second-hand knowledge, engraved as if witnessed. See M2, M5.

## W16. Recueil de Lions: Dessines d'apres nature par divers maitres et graves par B. Picart (Collection of Lions), 1729

Date 1729. Medium bound volume: 1 etched title, 1 engraved vignette on the printed title, 42 etched illustrations on 36 leaves of laid paper (30 full-page plates; 12 printed two etchings per page on 6 leaves). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found (volume collation only). Current location National Gallery of Art, Washington, accession 2019.8.1; Katharine Shepard Fund and Ailsa Mellon Bruce Fund. What it shows mechanically: lions drawn from nature by various masters, etched by Picart -- a natural-history album in which Picart is the engraver, not the designer. Mechanically it is the etched sketch mode: etching (rather than burin engraving) keeps the draughtsmen's loose, observed line, and the two-to-a-page leaves show the book as a study collection -- animals as specimens to compare, the page as a tray. It is the one documented Picart project that is frankly zoological rather than theological or mythological, and it shows his workshop functioning as a reproductive service for other artists' drawings. See M1 (the "d'apres nature" claim, here borrowed from others' drawings).

## W17. Hercules's Combat with the Hydra, 1731

Date 1731. Medium engraving. Dimensions platemark 14 x 10 1/8 in. (35.6 x 25.7 cm); sheet 19 1/4 x 12 in. (48.9 x 30.5 cm) -- VERIFIED (Georgia Museum of Art page read end to end, 19 lines). Current location Georgia Museum of Art, University of Georgia, object number 2001.176; gift of Phillip and Juanita Greenspan. What it shows mechanically: Hercules fighting the multi-headed Hydra -- a Temple of the Muses plate (Ovidian/mythological series). Mechanically it is the late mythological plate at full folio scale: a single muscular combat fills the plate, the Hydra's necks giving Picart a set of rising diagonals to braid against Hercules's body, the burin work dense in the foreground struggle and opening out behind. The large platemark (35.6 x 25.7 cm) against the even larger sheet shows the plate was issued with generous margins -- a display print inside a book, not a trimmed text illustration like W11.

## W18. Orpheus Leading Eurydice Out of Hell (Orpheus looks back and loses her forever), 1731

Date 1731 (plate dated; published in the Temple of the Muses, Amsterdam, 1733). Medium engraving (etching and engraving, per the source snippet). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimensions found. Current location not established (dealer snippet; no museum holding recovered). What it shows mechanically: Orpheus leading Eurydice out of Hades, turning back to look at her -- the instant of loss. One of the sixty full-page folio plates of Tafereel, of Beschryving van den prachtigen Tempel der Zang-Godinnen (Temple of the Muses). Mechanically it is the narrative-climax plate: the whole myth reduced to one turn of the head, the composition built as a passage (a dark mouth of hell behind, light ahead) with the backward glance as the hinge. Like W17 it sits inside the Temple's ornamental frame system (see W19).

## W19. Deucalion & Pyrrha Repeople the World (Deucalion & Pyrrha repeuplent la Terre), drawn 1731, published 1733

Date drawn 1731 (inscribed on the engraved frame, lower left: "B. Picart del. 1731"); published Amsterdam, 1733, in Le Temple des Muses (Tafereel... tempel der zang-godinnen). Medium etching and engraving on fine laid paper. Dimensions internal platemark 250 x 173 mm; external plate 356 x 257 mm; total (sheet) 490 x 307 mm -- reported-but-unverified (AbeBooks dealer page read end to end, 121 lines -- dealer page, so not VERIFIED). Current location not established (dealer listing, Antiquariaat Arine van der Steur, The Hague; seller inventory 59671). Other impressions in museum collections (not individually verified here). What it shows mechanically: after the flood, Deucalion and Pyrrha throw stones behind themselves; the stones become people as they touch the ground. Foreground left: Pyrrha throws large rocks; to the right, nude female figures of different ages emerge from the ground. Background: Deucalion repeats the action; a temple recalls their cry to Zeus; on the right the waters recede under clouds. Lettered in four languages below. The plate is composed as a picture inside a picture: a central narrative panel set inside a larger engraved frame "inhabited with details inspired to the central plate" -- the Temple of the Muses signature device. The three nested measurements (250 x 173 inner image; 356 x 257 outer plate; 490 x 307 sheet) record that frame-within-frame construction in numbers. Mechanically it is metamorphosis as assembly line: stones in, people out, the ground itself as the factory floor. See M5.

## W20. The Procession of Silenus, published 1734

Date published 1734, in Impostures innocentes (Amsterdam, widow of Bernard Picart), plate 2. Medium etching. Dimensions 21.5 x 29.6 cm (sheet of paper) -- reported-but-unverified (Royal Collection Trust snippet; the RCT object page itself returned only boilerplate and was NOT counted as read). Current location Royal Collection, Windsor, RCIN 851815; added to the Prince Consort's Raphael Collection c. 1853-76. A copy of the volume is RCIN 809004. What it shows mechanically: a drunken procession of Silenus and his revellers, reproducing (per the lettering below the image) a drawing then attributed to Raphael, housed in the Crozat Collection, Paris. The drawing is currently untraced. Mechanically it is the Impostures thesis in one plate: Picart engraves not a painting but a drawing, imitating the draughtsman's hand -- the etching mimics chalk/pen handling so closely that the print passes as the drawing's twin. The composition is better known through Agostino Veneziano's engraving after Raphael/Giulio Romano (RCIN 851816); Picart's version is a copy of a copy, and its claim is fidelity to the drawing's touch, not to Raphael's invention. A British Museum drawing of the same subject (1946,0713.502) is thought to be a copy after the original model for Agostino's engraving, not after Picart's print -- the attribution chain is tangled (see Flags). See M8.

## Beyond the 20

- **Les Plaisirs de la Jeunesse; A Picnic Party** -- early works illustrated on the Wikipedia page; no dates or dimensions recovered.
- **Portrait of Estienne (Etienne) Picart, who died after three days** -- early portrait, illustrated on Wikipedia; no date/dimensions recovered. (The "died after three days" caption is unexplained in the sources read -- it is the infant son Etienne, 15 May - 30 May 1713, see the life section; the Wikipedia caption's wording is garbled.)
- **Portrait of Bernard Picart** (mezzotint by Nicolaas Verkolje after Jean-Marc Nattier, 1715) -- a portrait OF Picart, not by him; frontispiece portrait by Jacobus van der Schley after Mattheus des Angles, 1734, for Impostures innocentes (British Museum 1901,1022.709, snippet; engraving, no dimensions on the snippet).
- **Brahma; Shiva & Ganesha; Matsya, Kurma, Varaha & Narasimha; Vamana, Parashurama, Rama & Krishna; Buddha & Kalki; Sati; Statues of Buddha in Thailand** -- Ceremonies gallery plates, 1723 (Wikipedia gallery). Hindu deity plates; the first European visual catalogue of Hinduism.
- **Quantecong, a Chinese Deity** -- Ceremonies plate (St Thomas Libraries blog, search snippet).
- **Florida Indians** -- six copper plates in Ceremonies Vol. 1 (of 30 engravings), after de Bry sources (Wikipedia; Sotheby's note: about 30 plates in Vols. I-II taken from de Bry's America).
- **Magician of Virginia / Priest of Virginia, seen from the right / from the left** -- 3 views on one sheet, after Theodor de Bry, dated 1721, Ceremonies Vol. 3 (1728) (dealer snippet).
- **Ligue de l'Allemagne, de l'Espagne et de la Hollande contre la France en 1672** -- Impostures Innocentes, plate 49; after Charles Le Brun (preparatory drawing for the Salon de la Paix, Versailles); c. 1724/33; etching; 206 x 407 mm (British Museum snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED; a wide, frieze-format plate.
- **Venus and Cupid** -- Impostures innocentes, plate 4; after a drawing then attributed to Raphael's workshop (copy of the Vatican stufetta fresco figures); on watermarked paper (RCT snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED by snippet; RCT page boilerplate only.
- **Sisyphus Pushing His Stone up a Mountain, 1731** -- Temple of the Muses plate (dealer snippet); no dimensions recovered.
- **Hell from the Temple of Muses, 1733** -- copper engraving, 35 x 25 cm (auction snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED; a dealer/auction snippet only.
- **Chimera Killed by Bellerophon** -- 18th-century engraving, Temple of the Muses series (dealer snippet); no dimensions recovered.
- **Le Temple des Muses (the volume), Amsterdam: Zacharias Chatelain, 1733** -- engraved title + 60 engraved plates (Christie's lot: a copy with 47 of 60 plates sold 13 Nov 1996 for USD 207; Lowell City Library provenance). Dutch 1733, English 1738, French 1742; captions in French, English, German, Dutch (STCN snippet). Artists involved: Michel de Marolles, Bernard Picart, Jacques Favereau, Abraham van Diepenbeeck, Cornelis Bloemaert (Wikipedia) -- Picart was not the sole designer.
- **Les Cesars de l'empereur Julien, 1728** -- over 300 medals and ancient monuments engraved by Picart (Wikipedia).
- **Superstitions anciennes et modernes, 1733-1736** -- Pierre Lebrun's book with Picart engravings, published by Jean-Frederic Bernard (Wikipedia).
- **Taferelen der voornaamste geschiedenissen van het Oude en Nieuwe Testament, 1728-1729** -- picture Bible, 214 large engravings (212 plates called for: Hoet 119, Houbraken 27, Picart 64; engraved by Duflos, Thomassin, Gouwen, Folkema and others); folio 470 x 330 mm (Sotheby's snippet) -- volume size, not a single plate. First edition with these illustrations, Pieter de Hondt, The Hague. Plates created 1705-1720, sold singly until collected in 1728.
- **Figures de la Bible, 1720** -- the picture-Bible suite initiated by Picart in 1720 with Gerard Hoet and Arnold Houbraken (Wikipedia; AbeBooks).
- **Reizen over Moskovie, door Persie en Indie (Voyages de Corneille le Brun), 1718** -- frontispiece with Cornelis de Bruyn (Wikipedia).
- **Histoire des grands chemins de l'empire romain (Bergier), title page, 1728** -- allegory on the road to Rome; etching and engraving, 220 x 159 mm (dealer snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED.
- **Henry of Orange** -- engraving on laid paper, 21.6 x 15.6 cm (Art Gallery of Ontario snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED.
- **Christ au Sepulcre** -- engraving on laid paper; image 15.7 x 10.9 cm (AGO snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED.
- **Canonisation of saints in 1712 (Ordre de la Procession... St Pierre, under Clement XI)** -- AFTER Bernard Picart, not by him; engraving, plate 32.5 x 54.5 cm (auction snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED; included to warn against attributing it to Picart himself.
- **Luitspeler (Lute Player)** -- Artera mirror, "1683-1733" (search snippet). Date range is meaningless (it spans his whole life); NOT used as a dated work. Artera-sourced: REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED by rule.
- **Net met fuiken en prikbord, 1731** -- Artera mirror, Rijksmuseum (search snippet). Artera-sourced: REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED by rule.
- **Doop en Avondmaal bij de Gereformeerden te Amsterdam, ca. 1730** -- Artera mirror, Rijksmuseum (search snippet). Artera-sourced: REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED by rule.
- **Twee putti met de tafelen der wet en een wierookvat** -- Artera mirror; dimensions height 83 mm x width 111 mm (search snippet). Artera-sourced: REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED by rule.
- **Impostures innocentes (the volume), Amsterdam: Veuve Bernard Picart, 1734** -- Met Museum object 45.131 (read end to end, 76 lines): engraved by Bernard Picart, written by Bernard Picart, after Raphael, Guido Reni, Nicolas Poussin, Carlo Maratti, Rembrandt; Books classification; Harris Brisbane Dick Fund, 1945. No dimensions on the Met page. See Flags for the plate-count dispute (78 vs 70).

## Evolution

**Paris period (to 1710/11).** Born rue Saint-Jacques, Paris, son of the engraver Etienne Picart ("le Romain"). At the Academie royale from 1689, taught by Charles Le Brun, Benoit Audran the Elder, Sebastien Leclerc and Antoine Coypel in the Wikipedia wording -- with the Le Brun prize story flagged as a misunderstanding by the specialist site (leg 1 F2; see C542). The 1704 Rubens plate (W1) is the Paris manner in miniature: large (49.5 x 34.6 cm, cut), reproductive, courtly -- a French academician translating a Flemish master's painting into burin, published in Paris under royal privilege ("Avec Privilege du Roy"). The early original work praised by contemporaries is also Parisian in spirit: the Slaughter of the Innocents (W3, 1715 -- painted in manner if not in place; the Cyclopedia judges "his best works are those executed in France"). McClintock & Strong's verdict (snippet-sourced) is blunt: in Amsterdam "he worked exclusively for the booksellers, and became mannered, metallic, and merely ornamental." That is a hostile 19th-century framing, but it names a real shift: from the large single reproductive plate to the book.

**The move (1710-1712).** Antwerp 1696, a year in Amsterdam, back to France end of 1698; first wife Claudine Prost and their children die; January 1710 he settles in The Hague with Prosper Marchand, in a "radical Huguenot coterie" reading John Locke; converts to Protestantism; moves to Amsterdam 1711, father follows; marries Anna Vincent in Haarlem, September 1712 (not April, as EN Wikipedia says -- see C549); becomes a citizen, joins the guild. The move is confessional and commercial at once: Amsterdam's French-language Huguenot book trade is the market, and Picart becomes its house engraver.

**Amsterdam period (1711-1733): the director, not the solitary burin.** Three manner changes are documented:

1. **From reproduction to direction.** The signature changes from "sculpsit" to "del. sculp. direxit" (W4, 1720: "B. Picart. del. sculp. direxit 1720"). He designs, engraves, and *directs* -- running a workshop and a school (pupils Jacob Folkema, Jakob van der Schley, Pieter Tanje, Francois Morellon la Cave, Johann Philipp Endelich; all used his drawings for engravings). Plates are executed by others from his designs (Fonbonne engraves W2; Duflos, Thomassin, Gouwen engrave the Bible plates). The Ceremonies credit line itself says "figures dessinees & gravees par Bernard Picard [sic.], & autres habiles artistes" -- Picart *and other skilled artists*.
2. **From the single plate to the system.** The Amsterdam work is serial: 214 Bible plates (1728), 70 gem plates (1724), 60 Temple of the Muses plates (1733), 266 Ceremonies engravings (EN Wikipedia's count), 42 lion etchings (1729). The unit shrinks (W11's image is 14.45 x 20.64 cm; W13 is 13.65 x 7.94 cm) and the meaning moves into the *set*: the two-tier plate (W10, W12), the two-images-on-one-plate economy (W11), the frame-within-frame (W19), the four-language caption (Temple of the Muses). The Ceremonies plates are the purest form: each rite is one entry in a comparative catalogue, and the catalogue is the work.
3. **From invention to fidelity -- in two opposite directions.** The gems (W7-W9) demand facsimile fidelity to tiny carved stones, enlarged so scholars can read them; the Impostures (W20) demand fidelity to other men's *hands* -- Raphael's drawing touch, Rembrandt's, Poussin's -- imitated so well the prints "are said to have deceived collectors" (Cyclopedia). Both are mimesis as virtuosity, but the gem plates erase the engraver and the Impostures plates flaunt him. Between them sits the Ceremonies method: observed ritual (W6, drawn from Amsterdam's Jews) and second-hand ritual (W15, from Tavernier's text) engraved with the *same* even-handed documentary finish, so that a witnessed circumcision and an imagined Tonkin funeral look equally authoritative. That evenness is the Enlightenment argument made visual -- and its danger.

**Late work (1728-1734).** The last five years are a rush of capstones: Les Cesars (1728, 300+ medals), Recueil de Lions (1729), the Temple of the Muses (plates drawn 1731, published 1733 -- the year he dies, 8 May 1733, buried Walloon Church 13 May), Superstitions (1733-36, finished posthumously), and Impostures innocentes (1734, published by his widow with his defence of reproductive printmaking, a biography, and a catalogue of his works). The Temple plates (W17-W19) are the most decorative thing he made in Amsterdam -- the narrative panel nested in an ornamental frame is a return to the Parisian large plate, but now the ornament is part of the book's system, repeated sixty times with variation. The Impostures is the self-conscious summation: a lifetime of imitating other masters, presented as a treatise on imitation itself.

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

- **Birth certificate not found.** "The birth certificate of Bernard Picart has, as far as I know, not yet been found. But there is no reason to doubt the accuracy of the information given in the 'Eloge historique'" (bernardpicart.net). Birth date/time (11 June 1673, 22:00-23:00) rests on the widow's biography alone (leg 1 F1).
