- **The Le Brun prize story is doubtful.** The Eloge says Picart won an Academy prize two years after entering in 1689, received from Le Brun; the specialist site: "This must have been a misunderstanding because the last award ceremony by Charles le Brun took place on 24 September 1689, and it is not likely that Picart received such a prestigious prize the same year he was admitted... Picart is, as far as I know, not mentioned as a prize winner in the documents concerning the history of the Academie, and the drawing with which he won the prize is not known". Formal reception as a member is not established either (leg 1 F2/F17; see C542).
- **England trip is a mistake.** "It has been suggested Picart went to England, but this is a mistake based on an account in a letter of September 1698 written by Daniel Cronstrom to Nicodeme Tessin le Jeune". Do not repeat the England trip (leg 1 F3).
- **First marriage: name spelling, marriage month, and the "twins".** (a) First wife: "Claudine Prost" (specialist site; FR Wikipedia; NL Wikipedia) vs EN Wikipedia "Cloudina Pros". (b) Second marriage: EN Wikipedia "In April 1712 he married Anna Vincent... in Haarlem" vs archival record -- notice of intended marriage 9 September 1712, marriage 25 September 1712 in Haarlem. April 1712 is wrong on the archival evidence. (c) Children of the second marriage: EN Wikipedia "male twins... both died within a few weeks; he portrayed them both" vs the specialist site's single son Etienne, born 15 May 1713, buried 30 May 1713, portrait inscribed "Etiene Picart mort a trois jours". The twins claim is not corroborated; the single-son record, with a surviving red-chalk portrait in the Rijksmuseum (RP-T-1951-77), is the stronger evidence (leg 1 F4; see C549).
- **Anna Vincent's death year.** EN Wikipedia: 1684-1736; specialist site text: died 5 March 1736; specialist site header: "Anna Vincent (1684-1737?)". 1736 is the better-supported year; the "1737?" is the site's own hedge, unresolved (leg 1 F5/F10).
- **Knights of Jubilation vs Freemasonry.** Margaret Jacob's suggestion of a Masonic link is rejected by Berkvens-Stevelinck, who "shows convincingly that this company has nothing to do with Freemasonry". Do not present the Knights of Jubilation as proto-Masonic (leg 1 F6).
- **Amsterdam settlement: 1710 vs 1711.** The resolution is geographic, not calendrical: The Hague January 1710, Amsterdam 1711. Sources that say "settled in Amsterdam in 1710" (Encyclopedia.com; Sotheby's; McClintock & Strong) are loose against the archival sequence (leg 1 F7/F28; see C550).
- **Ceremonies plate and volume counts disagree across sources.** Plates: 243 (Sotheby's) vs "more than 250" (Nellis; UCLA) vs 263 (Zocalo review, snippet) vs 266 (EN Wikipedia). Volumes: seven folio volumes 1723-1737 plus two added by Bernard (Sotheby's) vs "ten volumes of text" (EN Wikipedia) vs seven volumes "with supplements eventually reaching 12 volumes" (GAMEO) vs 11 volumes in-folio 1723-43 (FR Wikipedia). Nellis's note 1 explains part of it: "The order of the original French edition of the Ceremonies varies from set to set". No single count can be stated without naming the edition/set. Impostures innocentes has the same problem: 78 engravings (niceartgallery; FR Wikipedia) vs "70 copper-engraved plates numbered 1-67 and A-L" (Invaluable auction description, snippet) vs FR Wikipedia's claim the volume appeared in 1738 (against the Met's 1734 and the standard 1734 date). The 78 may count portrait, title and frontispiece; unresolved in this leg (leg 1 F8; leg 2 flag 5; see C543).
- **Pupil list: two incompatible rosters.** EN Wikipedia (via RKD for Endelich): Folkema, Van der Schley, Tanje, Morellon la Cave, Endelich. FR Wikipedia adds Joseph Aved, Claude Dubosc, Louis Fabricius Dubourg, Edme Jeaurat, Louis Surugue, Henri Simon Thomassin. The French names are likely Paris-period pupils; the Amsterdam school roster is the shorter one. The "seven students" who assisted on the Ceremonies are never named as a set in any source read. Do not present either list as complete (leg 1 F9).
- **Widow's inventory date.** EN Wikipedia: "Her inventory (on 12 March 1736)". Specialist site, citing Stadsarchief Amsterdam Notaris Ardinois 9126, 347: "The estate inventory was made up on 17 April 1736". The 12 March date may be a different document (testament? valuation?) or an error; unresolved. The "around 400 portfolios" and "54 paintings" figures are consistent across both (leg 1 F11; see C544).
- **"Whether Picart really painted is not assured."** RKD, via genealogieonline snippet. RKD lists him as painter/miniaturist among his qualifications, but with this caveat. Do not call him a painter without the hedge; his surviving work is drawing and printmaking. Also FR Wikipedia's infobox lists "Formation: Stadstekenacademie" -- that is the Amsterdam drawing academy where genealogieonline says he was a teacher ("leraar aan de Stadstekenacademie"), not a student; the infobox is likely wrong or misleading. Unverified either way (leg 1 F12).
- **La Circoncision des Juifs Portugais date: 1722 vs 1725.** Nellis's figure caption says 1722; her text says "the 1725 engraving entitled La CIRCONCISION des JUIFS PORTUGAIS"; the Rijksmuseum object is cited at the same handle in both places. One of the two dates is a slip; the plate belongs to the first Ceremonies volume (1723) either way (leg 1 F14; leg 2 flag 1).
- **Temple of the Muses authorship.** FR/EN Wikipedia list Michel de Marolles, Picart, Jacques Favereau, Abraham van Diepenbeeck and Cornelis Bloemaert as "the artists involved" -- these are the designers of the source compositions (17th-century), not the engravers of the 1733 plates; STCN says "Engraved by B. Picart le Romain et al". Do not present Marolles et al. as co-engravers. Also: the 1733 Dutch edition is the first; the French edition is 1742 (not 1733), English 1738 (leg 1 F15; leg 2 flag 3).
- **The "1,300 prints" total.** McClintock & Strong (1870): "His prints altogether amount to about 1300" (snippet-sourced); FR Wikipedia: the Catalogue general in Impostures is "compose de plus de 1 300 planches". Both trace to the same 1734 catalogue; treat as a catalogue count, not a modern census. The Rijksmuseum "2,000+ works online" counts impressions, states and related objects, not distinct compositions (leg 1 F16/F24; see C545).
- **Gemmae dates: 1722 vs 1723 vs 1724.** Individual plates are dated 1722 (Bacchus and Ariadne, pl. XXII, dealer listing) and 1723 (BM gem plates W8/W9); the volume was published Amsterdam: Bernard Picart, 1724 (Swann). Plate dates precede volume publication -- normal for a serial project, but do not cite 1724 as the date of a single gem plate (leg 2 flag 6).
- **Tonquin date (W15): plate 1729 vs volume reference 1728.** The plate is inscribed 1729; 1stDibs notes the French-volume reference carries a 1728 date. The inscription dates the plate; the volume date does not (leg 2 flag 7).
- **Inca plate attribution (W10).** The SuperStock listing attributes it "Bernard Picart (workshop or), After Bernard Picart" -- i.e. possibly workshop execution after his design, not his own hand. Do not present it as autograph without qualification (leg 2 flag 8).
- **"Le Romain" belongs to the father.** Etienne Picart was "le Romain" for his time in Rome; Bernard is also styled "B. Picart le Romain" on title pages (e.g. Les Cesars, 1728). The nickname does not distinguish father from son -- check which Picart a "le Romain" imprint means (leg 2 flag 9).
- **Picart vs Picard spelling.** "Picard" appears in period imprints and in the Sotheby's title transcription ("Bernard Picard [sic.]"); the standard modern form is Picart (leg 2 flag 10).
- **Procession of Silenus attribution chain (W20).** The drawing Picart reproduced (then attributed to Raphael, Crozat Collection) is untraced; Pouncey/Gere (1962) warned the British Museum drawing of the subject cannot be identified with the drawing Picart reproduced. The plate is a facsimile of a lost attributed drawing, itself related to Agostino Veneziano's engraving -- three removes from Raphael (leg 2 flag 11).
- **Robinson Crusoe second plate (W4).** A second plate of the same design exists without the Picart credit line, used for subsequent editions; the Dutch edition redacted the plate. Not every 1720 Crusoe frontispiece is Picart's plate, even when the design is his (leg 2 flag 12).
- **Artera dates are unreliable.** "Luitspeler" is dated "1683-1733" on Artera -- a range spanning his entire life, i.e. no date at all. Artera dimensions/dates are REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED by rule and were not used for any of the 20 works (leg 2 flag 13).
- **"Best works executed in France" (Cyclopedia).** A 19th-century critical judgement, not a fact about the work. Noted here because it shapes the early/late framing; the Amsterdam plates' documentary and serial innovations are the counter-evidence (leg 2 flag 14).
- **"More than 2,000 works" at the Rijksmuseum.** Repeated from Wikipedia. Not independently verified in this leg -- no Rijksmuseum listing was browsed (leg 2 flag 15; see C545).
- **Chain claim unverified in this leg.** The brief states Picart "is the trunk of the Amsterdam engraving line Vinkeles inherited". No source read in this leg traces the Picart-to-Vinkeles descent (Vinkeles was born 1741, eight years after Picart's death; the link runs through pupils like Van der Schley and Tanje and the Amsterdam school tradition). The descent needs its own evidence; do not assert it as sourced fact in the study (leg 1 F26; see C548).
- **FR Wikipedia infobox oddities.** "Periode d'activite: 1732" is unexplained and plainly incomplete for a career spanning 1693-1733; "Conjoint: ... Anna Vincent (remariage en 1711)" conflicts with the archival September 1712 marriage. Treat the FR infobox as unreliable on dates (leg 1 F27).
- **Key modern sources NOT read end to end.** Baskind 2007 ("Bernard Picart's Etchings of Amsterdam's Jews", Jewish Social Studies 13.2, Winter 2007): abstract only; its argument (Picart's imagery as a critique of the Ashkenazi influx) is noted but not verified from the text. The Book That Changed Europe (Hunt, Jacob & Mijnhardt, 2010): not read; used via reviews/summaries. The Cambridge "Transnational Crusoe" Element (2025): read only in part (lines 0-119 of 1,624). The RKD Artists entry (ID 63267) was never opened directly. (leg 1 F20-F23).
- **No work inspected at usable image resolution.** Every work in this leg is known from text descriptions, captions, dimensions and signatures in the pages read. The Princeton page links four thumbnail images and the Nellis PDF reproduces seven figures, but the text fetches do not deliver images at a resolution that supports visual claims about line, tone or composition. Any claim in this study about *how* a plate looks rests on texts, not on looking; a later leg should view the plates directly (Rijksmuseum image URLs are cited in leg 1: e.g. http://hdl.handle.net/10934/RM0001.COLLECT.308497 for the circumcision plate) (leg 1 F25).

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

- VERIFIED (museum or auction page read end to end): **5 in the 20** -- W1, W5, W11, W13, W17.
- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (dimensions from search snippets or dealer/AI-mirror pages only): **7** -- W2, W6, W8, W9, W14, W15, W19.
- NOT VERIFIED (no dimensions found anywhere): **8** -- W3, W4, W7, W10, W12, W16, W18, W20.
- Total: 5 + 7 + 8 = 20.

## Aesthetic logic

Picart's aesthetic is the logic of the encyclopaedist, not the logic of the original. Everything in his system serves one question: how does a hand that can draw anything decide what to draw, and how does it keep drawing it for twenty-two Amsterdam years without ever leaving the city? The signature "d'apres nature" answers first: the claim "I was there" is engraved into the plate, and a ceremony rendered as a specific room with specific people in contemporary dress is worth more than any inherited tableau. The key answers second: a crowd scene with a legend beneath it is a legible system, every figure given a role, and the roles are the picture. The pair answers third: show the ritual, then show the instruments -- the wide shot and the macro teach what neither plate alone can. The collage answers fourth: keep the familiar composition, swap in the observed fact, and the borrowed scaffolding carries the correction. The staging answers fifth: a ceremony is not illustrated, it is staged, the viewer seated in the audience and shown the stage machinery too -- and the Temple's frame-within-a-frame is the same device turned inward, a stage inside a stage. The small piece answers sixth: the masterpiece can be 12 cm wide, delicacy of finish is the ambition, and the vignette is where the burin is most delicate. The title print answers seventh: the frontispiece is the book's soul -- it must delight, honour, sell, and be explained, because the book is a body and the print animates it. The imitation answers eighth: a copy so exact it fools the connoisseur is not forgery but authorship, and the reproductive print is an art with its own status. The two tools answer ninth: the etched line sketches, the burin finishes, and their spacing stands in for colour. The gilded plate answers tenth: the edition ends by turning its own matrix into a keepsake, the tool of reproduction retired as a wedding present. The self-printing answers eleventh: the print is only finished when the artist has pulled it himself, on chosen paper, under his own eyes, because the publisher's impression is a different, lesser object. And the school answers last: the atelier is a machine with two gears -- Picart invents, the school executes -- and he reserves his own burin for the pieces that carry his name most closely, the small delicate ones, while the large survey work goes out under his direction. What holds it all together is the book: the Ceremonies is not a collection of plates but a single machine for comparing religions, each rite one entry in a catalogue, and the catalogue is the work. His palette logic follows: there is no palette, only density -- and his tragedy is the one the French tradition states plainly, that the booksellers pressed him into cold, licked work, he earned a great deal of money at the cost of his reputation, and the connoisseurs valued only his early, careful works, while the widow's Eloge insists he was a perfectly honest man, more worthy of the esteem of honest people than of the approval of connoisseurs.

## Positions

**P1 -- Comparison on equal footing.** The Ceremonies presents all religions "as even-handed as possible" and "sought to capture the ritual and ceremonial life of all the known religions of the world"; Hunt, Jacob and Mijnhardt argue it "sowed the radical idea that religions could be considered on equal terms". The stance is editorial as much as visual: Judaism first in volume one, "idolatrous peoples" treated as possessing religion rather than lacking it.

**P2 -- Witnessing over inheriting.** Where earlier images stereotyped, Picart went and looked: synagogue, Seder table, circumcision instruments drawn "d'apres nature" (M1). Nellis: his depiction "clearly stems from a more engaged involvement with the matter represented" than Leusden's or de Hooghe's. The stance: an image earned by presence beats an image inherited from the archive.

**P3 -- The reproductive print is an art, with its own authorship.** The Discours sur les prejuges de certains curieux touchant la gravure (1732) defends interpretive engraving; the Impostures prove that a faithful reproduction can be an authentic Picart (M8; Q10). The stance is a professional claim: the engraver is not a copyist but an author working in a second medium.

**P4 -- Knowledge must be sold as well as told.** Bernard's January 1721 Journal des Scavans advertisement stresses the new typeface, the most beautiful paper, sample pages on view in the Kalverstraat shop; Lairesse's third requirement for a title print is that it be "advantageous to the seller" (M7). The stance: the Enlightenment book is a commodity, and its beauty is part of its argument.

**P5 -- Leave Paris rather than serve it badly.** He left France because of his criticism of Roman ritual *and* because "in France at that moment there was little interesting work for (reproductive) printmakers"; he then refused a job cataloguing the Order of the Saint-Esprit arms because it was not appropriate work and had already been promised to his teacher Leclerc. The stance: the work must be worthy of the hand, or the hand goes elsewhere.

**P6 -- Small and finished over grand and hasty.** The Eloge's praise centres on "small pieces... of a soft & sociable character", and the French tradition records that connoisseurs valued only his early, careful works once the flood of Amsterdam commissions made him "mannered" (McClintock & Strong: "became mannered, metallic, and merely ornamental", snippet-sourced). The stance is visible in what he kept for himself: the Epithalames are small, signed invenit et sculpsit, and printed under his own eyes (M11, M12).

**P7 -- Anonymity as a tool.** The Great Mirror of Folly was published "without an author or a publisher listed, although many now connect the volume largely to Picart"; the Robinson Crusoe frontispiece exists in a second state with Picart's credit line removed at the publishers' request. The stance: the name appears where it adds value and disappears where the market or the satire requires it.

**P8 -- The engraver as publisher-entrepreneur.** Citizen (1713), guild member as art dealer (1713), owner of his own print shop, seller to the curieux on chosen paper with wide margins. The stance: control the plate, the proof, the paper and the sale -- the artist as his own bookseller.

**P9 -- Sympathy for the persecuted and the marginal.** EN Wikipedia: "Because of the many prints it also seems he sympathized with Jansenists, the Armenian Apostolic Church and Collegiants". The Ceremonies gives sympathetic portraits of Jews, Mennonites/Anabaptists, Quakers, and "idolatrous peoples". The stance is confessional without being sectarian: the persecuted get the careful plate.

**P10 -- French training, Dutch practice.** "He actually introduced his French rooted ideas about the training of printmakers in the Netherlands" -- the Academie discipline of drawing, perspective and architecture (Leclerc) transplanted into an Amsterdam workshop school (M12). Benezit's verdict, quoted in FR Wikipedia: "Picart realisa, en quelque sorte, une synthese du gout hollandais et de l'esprit francais, et l'on peut dire qu'il fut le representant le plus remarquable de la gravure hollandaise du premier tiers du XVIIIe siecle, influence par l'Ecole francaise" (see Q11). The stance: carry the Paris method into the Amsterdam market and let the mixture be the style.

## Quotes

Q1-Q2 are the Eloge historique (Prosper Marchand, via Anna Vincent). Q3 is the memorial portrait's allegorical explanation. Q4 is Anna Vincent. Q5 is Jean-Frederic Bernard. Q6 is a title-print poem about Picart. Q7 is the widow's Eloge. Q8 is ABOUT him (snippet-sourced). Q9 is Jonathan I. Israel. Q10 is Ann Jensen Adams. Q11 is Beneal. Q12 is Picart and Marchand's joint declaration -- the closest this study comes to Picart's own words. Q13 is Daniel Cronstrom. Q14 is Picart's self-styled signature. No quote in this study is Picart's own words alone.

- **Q1** (ABOUT, the Eloge historique, closing praise, in the specialist site's translation): "Finally, allow me to end with this small but just praise, which was given to him by one of his intimate friends [undoubtedly Prosper Marchand]. Everyone knows, and most of his works prove, that he excelled in the beautiful invention & the beautiful order of the subjects that he had to deal with, the engraving of small pieces, like his epithalames, his vignettes, his cul-de-lampe/tail pieces, of a soft & sociable character, only occupied with his study & his homework. Good citizen, good friend, good head of family: in one word, a perfectly honest man, even more worthy of the esteem of honest people, than of the approval of connoisseurs."
- **Q2** (ABOUT, the same passage in the French original): "Tout le monde sait, et la pluspart de ses ouvrages prouvent, qu'il exelloit dans la belle invention et la belle ordonnance des sujets qu'il avoit a traiter, dans l'exactitude et la correction du dessein, et principalement dans la delicatesse et la proprete de la gravure des petites pieces, comme ses epithalames, ses vignettes, ses culs-de-lampe, ses titres de livres, etc. Mais plus que tout cela, c'etoit un homme de moeurs tres reglees, d'un caractere doux et sociable, uniquement o[c]cupe de son etude et de ses devoirs, bon citoyen, bon ami, bon chef de famille: en un mot, un parfaitement honnete-homme, plus digne encore de l'estime des honnetes-gens, que de l'approbation des connoisseurs."
- **Q3** (ABOUT, the memorial portrait's allegorical explanation): "L'histoire tenant negligemment un livre ferme, & tristement accoudee sur un table aupres de sa plume qu'elle a comme abbandonne, paroit inconsolable de ce que les plus beaux de ses monumens ne seront plus consacrez au temple de la memoire par cette illustre dessinateur. Le Genie du dessein penetre de douleur, deplore amerement sa porte, & celui de la gravure tache de la consoler en lui faisant voir la couronne de l'immortalitee que des beaux ouvrages de ce grand maitre lui ont si legitiment acquise."
- **Q4** (BY, Anna Vincent, Avertissement to Impostures innocentes): "Et pour ne rien oublier de ce qui pouvoit contribuer a la satisfaction du public, & pour honnorer la memoire de ce cher defunt, j'ai mis son portrait a la tete de son eloge historique. Ce portrait est tres ressemblant. On acheva de peindre mr. Picart, environ un mois avant qu'il tombe malade, & dans un tems qu'il se portoit parfaitement bien."
- **Q5** (BY, Jean-Frederic Bernard, Journal des Scavans, January 1721, announcing the Ceremonies): "Le Libraire s'engage aussi de son cote a ne donner au Public rien qui ne puisse lui etre utile, & a ne faire engraver que des Figures instructives & necessaires, dont le dessein soit beau & correct: car on se propose d'expliquer, & de donner une idee Claire des sujets qu'on traite, autant pour les moins que de satisfaire la vue, qui ne laissera pas d'y trouver son compte."
- **Q6** (ABOUT, Johannes Oosterwyk, title-print poem for the eighth Epithalame (1723), on drawing teaching the etching needle): "De Tekenkunst, die van natuur | Ontfong haar leven, kragt, en vuur, | Leert de Etsnaald met veel losse zwieren | Op 't gladde koper zich bestieren, | Dus brengt *Picart* in dit taff'reel | Te voorschyn een volmaakt prieel | Van Liefde, en Weelde; een Huuwlyks Eden, | En Tempe vol vermaaklykheden, | Getooit met jeugdig Lentegroen."
- **Q7** (ABOUT, the widow's Eloge, quoted in FR Wikipedia's critical reception): "C'etait un homme assidu et infatigable et, si l'on peut dire, amoureux de son art. Toute sa recreation etait de dessiner et, lors meme qu'il avait compagnie chez lui, il s'eclipsait tout doucement et regagnait son cabinet. Il ne faisait presque rien que d'apres nature, tant dans ses sujets modernes que dans ses sujets heroiques, et il n'en traitait aucun, soit fabuleux, soit historique, qu'il n'eut attentivement examine les auteurs qui pouvaient l'en instruire... Il a fait un nombre incroyable d'etudes d'apres nature, tant en figures d'academie qu'en draperies; il portait toujours des tablettes sur lui, ainsi il tirait parti de tout: la mer, la campagne, le paysage, les plantes, la ville, la foule, les foires, les voitures publiques... On ne croirait pas avec quel soin il composait et finissait tous les dessins qu'il devait graver."
- **Q8** (ABOUT, McClintock & Strong, Cyclopedia of Biblical, Theological and Ecclesiastical Literature (1870) -- SNIPPET-SOURCED, not read end to end): "Having embraced the Reformed religion, he took up his residence in Holland. In Amsterdam, to which place he accompanied his father in 1710, he worked exclusively for the booksellers, and became mannered, metallic, and merely ornamental."
- **Q9** (ABOUT, Jonathan I. Israel on the Ceremonies, quoted in EN Wikipedia): "an immense effort to record the religious rituals and beliefs of the world in all their diversity as objectively and authentically as possible"
- **Q10** (ABOUT, Ann Jensen Adams, "Reproduction and authenticity in Bernard Picart's 'Impostures innocentes'", quoted in FR Wikipedia): "Picart stands at the cusp of a change in attitude toward the reproductive print, a time in which reproductive printmakers were faced with an impossible contradiction: they were required to faithfully convey information about the original and at the same time no lose sight of their own personnality and status as craftsmen. Picart's etchings argue for the status (and market value) of reproductive engravings in general and of those produced by their author in particular, treading a fine line beetween reproduction and authenticity. In the process of creating faithful reproductions of other artists, he creates authentic Picarts." [sic: "no lose" and "beetween" as rendered in FR Wikipedia.]
- **Q11** (ABOUT, Beneal, Dictionnaire des peintres, sculpteurs, dessinateurs et graveurs, quoted in FR Wikipedia): "Heritier de la tradition de Jacques Callot, il dessine avec precision et meme parfois avec minutie, se laissant aller a une certaine forme d'elegance soutenue par une vivacite technique qui annonce l'esprit du XVIIIe siecle, en particulier de Gillot et de Watteau. Picart realisa, en quelque sorte, une synthese du gout hollandais et de l'esprit francais, et l'on peut dire qu'il fut le representant le plus remarquable de la gravure hollandaise du premier tiers du XVIIIe siecle, influence par l'Ecole francaise."
- **Q12** (BY, Picart and Marchand's declaration on joining the Walloon church, The Hague, 14 April 1710): "Dieu leur ayant fait de la grace, par la lecture de sa Sainte Parole, et de plusieurs bons livres, de connoitre les erreurs de la Religion Romaine, ils ont renonce des il y a plusieurs annees a ses assemblees et a son culte superstitieux et idolatre, se nourrissant entre eux et avec plusieurs autres de la bonne pature, en puisant la verite dans les sources pures, de sorte qu'enfin sur les soupcons qu'on a pris de leurs sentimens, ils ont ete en danger d'etre arretez, et obligez pour jouir d'une entiere liberte de sortir leur Paris."
- **Q13** (ABOUT, Daniel Cronstrom to Nicodemus Tessin Jr., September 1696, comparing Picart and Benoit Audran): "Tous deux sont d'un genie et d'une reputation a augmenter tous les jours, et tous deux tres honnestes gens, et tous les deux dans la vigueur de leur age. Si vous me donner le choix, j'entreroy dans un examen encore plus detaille de leur fort et faible, et je ne feroy rien dont je craigne des reproches ce que je scais a present. Le Picard est plus routine dans le dessein des figures a la Vandermeule que l'autre; pour la graveure, Mr. Audran a un peu plus d'acquit, mais le Piccard est si fort employe aussi que sa reputation et son scavoir augmentent tous les jours."
- **Q14** (BY, Picart's signature as "barbouilleur et enlumineur" of the Knights of Jubilation, 24 November 1710): "Bernard Picart, barbouilleur et enlumineur de l'ordre" -- with Prosper Marchand as "secretaire de l'ordre".

## Network

Teachers: 1. Etienne Picart le Romain (1632-1721) -- father, first teacher, graveur du roi; Rome 1655-61 with Guillaume Vallet; Academie Royale member 1664; last years from 1710 in the Netherlands; joined his son in Amsterdam. FLAG: dates conflict -- GAMEO 1632-1721; British Museum 1631-1721; NGV born 21 Oct 1632, died 12 Nov 1721. His own teacher was Gilles Rousselet (not Francois de Poilly, per the British Museum) -- second-order, not counted. Proposed as main 3 for #545. 2. Charles Le Brun (1619-1690) -- Picart "taught by Charles le Brun" (EN Wikipedia Bernard Picart). FLAG: bernardpicart.net says the prize story is a misunderstanding -- Le Brun's last award ceremony was 24 Sept 1689 and Picart is not listed as a prize winner in Academie documents. The teacher link stands on Wikipedia; the prize anecdote does not. Dates 1619-1690 from a search-result book citation, not re-verified against a page read end to end. Proposed as spare 1 for #545. 3. Benoit Audran the Elder (1661-1721) -- Picart's first instructions came from his father and Benoit Audran, whose drawings he mimicked young (bernardpicart.net). Second son of Germain Audran; studied under uncle Gerard Audran; Academie 1709; engraver to the king. FLAG: dates conflict -- Wikipedia 23 Nov 1661 - 3 Sept 1721 vs Prabook 22 Nov 1661 - 2 Oct 1721. Proposed as spare 2 for #545. 4. Sebastien Leclerc (1637-1714) -- Picart studied architecture and perspective under Leclerc (bernardpicart.net). Joined the Academie 1672, professor of geometry and perspective; graveur du roi. FLAG: internal Wikipedia conflict -- bapt. 26 Sept 1637, died 2 Oct 1714 (infobox) vs 25 Oct 1714 (article body). Proposed as spare 3 for #545.

Family: 5. Cloudina Pros / Cloudine Prost (dates NOT established) -- first wife, daughter of a bookseller; she and their children died by 1708. Spelling varies (Picart article "Cloudina Pros"; genealogieonline "Cloudine Prost"). 6. Anna Vincent (1684-1736) -- second wife, married September 1712 in Haarlem (not April, as EN Wikipedia says); three daughters baptized at the Westerkerk; son Etienne May 1713 died within weeks (not male twins -- see C549); as widow published Impostures innocentes (1734) and Pierre Lebrun's Superstitions anciennes et modernes (1733-36); her inventory of 17 April 1736 (not 12 March) listed ~400 portfolios of copperplates and 54 paintings. 7. Ysbrand Vincent (1642-1718) -- father-in-law, rich paper seller. 8. Levinus Vincent (1658 - 8 Nov 1727) -- wife's uncle, Mennonite, rich designer and merchant of damask, silk and brocade; cabinet of curiosities (Wondertooneel der Nature, 1706-1715); visitor book 1705-1737 with 3,500+ entries including Peter the Great (visited 1697). FLAG: Wikipedia calls him "Levinus Vincent the Younger" (an elder is implied, not identified); the visitor book runs to 1737, ten years past his death -- it was evidently continued after him. Frontispiece to his book by Romeyn de Hooghe (second-order, not counted). Proposed as spare 5 for #545. 9. Anna Yver (dates NOT established) -- mother-in-law; lived on the Rokin 1723/1726.

Paris circle and influences: 10. Antoine Coypel (1661-1722, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- named in Wikipedia's "taught by Charles le Brun, along with Benoit Audran the Elder, Sebastien Leclerc and Antoine Coypel". FLAG: the phrasing is ambiguous -- Coypel may be a fellow pupil of Le Brun rather than a teacher of Picart; counted here as Paris-circle fellow pupil, not as a teacher. Proposed as spare 6 for #545. 11. Francois Girardon (dates NOT established in this run; commonly 1628-1715, unverified) -- sculptor of the Richelieu tomb/memorial; Picart's first proud assignment was two drawings of it, followed by a price dispute; the engravings went to Charles Simonneau. 12. Charles Simonneau (dates NOT established) -- engraver who received the Richelieu tomb plates after Picart's price dispute. 13. Charles Levier (dates NOT established) -- member of the radical Huguenot coterie with Picart and Marchand in The Hague (1710), studying John Locke. No dates found in 20 searches; held back as a candidate for that reason. 14. Jean Claude (dates NOT established in this run) -- Huguenot theologian, named as an influence on Picart's circle. 15. Pierre Bayle (dates NOT established in this run) -- philosopher, named as an influence on Picart's circle.

Amsterdam collaborators and book trade: 16. Jean Frederic Bernard (1683-1744) -- Huguenot publisher; wrote most of the Ceremonies text (uncredited); Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses de tous les peuples du monde (1723-1743), 10 volumes, 266 engravings, ~3,000 copies sold, banned by the Catholic Church 1738; 7 volumes 1723-1737 plus 2 further volumes. FLAG: HathiTrust gives his birth as "1683?" -- the year carries a question mark. Already carried as #542 extra spare -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed. 17. Jacobus Houbraken (25 December 1698 - 14 November 1780) -- picture-bible collaborator; also engraved the portrait of Nicolaas Verkolje ca. 1752. Already carried as #542 main -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed except as #545 top pick (see Part 2 of leg 3 / Future candidates). Dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3 (57 lines). 18. Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733) -- picture-bible illustrator: 119 of the 214 designs (the largest share). Trained by his father Moses Hoet (glass painter) and Warnard van Rijsen (pupil of Cornelis van Poelenburgh); The Hague 1672, Rees/Cleves/Paris, Utrecht; founded a drawing academy 1697 with Hendrick Schoock; book on drawing 1712 (103 prints by Pieter Bodart); source for Houbraken's Bentvueghels list; art dealer with Jacques Ignatius de Roore. Dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3 (185 lines). FLAG: a White Rose header gives "Zaltbommel 1669" -- apparently wrong against born Zaltbommel 22 Aug 1648. FLAG: the Hoet article says Van Gool's book "was published in 1650" -- an evident error for 1750, since it also says prints by Jacob Houbraken illustrate its first volume. Proposed as main 2 for #545. 19. Arnold Houbraken (1660-1719) -- picture-bible illustrator: 27 of the 214 designs; author of De Groote Schouburgh (1718-21), which his son Jacobus helped finish. Already carried as #535 spare -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed. 20. Cornelis de Bruyn (1652-1727) -- traveller-painter; collaborated with Picart on the frontispiece of Reizen over Moskovie (1718; French ed. Voyages de Corneille le Brun, Amsterdam: freres Wetstein, 1718), the frontispiece signed "B. Picart delineavit ano 1711"; 320+ copper plates; Persepolis engravings. FLAG: dates conflict -- HathiTrust 1652-1727 vs Sotheby's "1652-1726/7". Proposed as alternate 2 for #545. 21. Philipp von Stosch (22 March 1691 - 7 Nov 1757) -- Prussian antiquarian, Rome/Florence; Gemmae Antiquae Caelatae (1724), 70 antique hardstones, engraved and published by Picart; collection 10,000+ cameos/intaglios/glass pastes, mostly to Berlin museums; Winckelmann catalogued it 1760. Stosch originally employed Hieronymus Odam, whom Picart replaced; accused of involvement in forged gems (2025 article). Proposed as spare 4 for #545. 22. Hieronymus Odam (1681-1741) -- Stosch's first gem illustrator, replaced by Picart. Proposed as extra verified spare for #545. 23. Prosper Marchand (11 March 1678 - 14 June 1756) -- French bibliographer/bookseller; Paris shop rue Saint-Jacques under the Phenix (with Gabriel II Martin, second-order); fled to Holland winter 1709; The Hague refuge with Picart Jan 1710, Amsterdam the year after; abandoned bookselling c.1713; proofreader for Fritsch & Bohm, Rotterdam; editor Journal litteraire 1713-1737; Dictionnaire historique (1758-59, posthumous); London 1726-1735; left his papers to Leiden University Library; with Picart and pupil Jakob van der Schley arranged illustrations for numerous books (Marchand supplying descriptions and rough sketches). FLAG: moved to Holland 1709 (Wikipedia) vs 1711 (Alchetron). Proposed as reserve for #545. 24. Zacharias Chatelain (dates NOT established) -- publisher of Temple of the Muses (Amsterdam 1733; further editions 1742, 1749). Held back as a candidate: no dates established. 25. Antoine de La Barre de Beaumarchais (1698 - c.1757) -- author of the Temple of the Muses text (retelling of classical legends with extensive notes and commentary); native of Cambrai; canon regular of Saint Victor, left the order, worked in the book trade in the Netherlands (Leiden, The Hague), then Hamburg and Frankfurt. FLAG: birth 1698 from Deutsche Biographie only; death "d. c. 1757" (Bonhams) / "?-~1757" (Turrettini). Not to be confused with Pierre-Augustin Caron de Beaumarchais (1732-1799), a different person. Proposed as extra verified spare for #545. 26. Michel de Marolles (1600-1681, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- abbe; his 1655 Temple des Muses is the work Beaumarchais's text and Picart's plates rework. 27. Abraham van Diepenbeeck (dates NOT established in this run; commonly 1596-1675, unverified) -- the 1655 Temple des Muses illustrations were designed by him; Picart's 1733 plates rework his designs. 28. Jacques Favereau (dates NOT established) -- engraver on the Temple of the Muses. Held back as a candidate: no dates established. 29. Pieter de Hondt (1696-1764) -- publisher of the picture bible Taferelen der voornaamste geschiedenissen van het Oude en Nieuwe Testament (The Hague, 1728-29). Proposed as extra verified spare for #545. 30. Jacques Saurin (dates NOT established) -- wrote the descriptive text of the Taferelen picture bible. Held back as a candidate: no dates established. 31. Pierre Lebrun (1661-1729) -- Oratorian theologian; Histoire critique des pratiques superstitieuses (1702); his and Jean-Baptiste Thiers's work was reissued by Picart's widow as Superstitions anciennes et modernes (Amsterdam: J.-F. Bernard, 1733-36), often counted as volumes 10-11 of the Ceremonies. FLAG: AbeBooks lists him as "1661-1723" -- an outlier against HathiTrust and encyclopedia.com (1661-1729). Proposed as extra verified spare for #545. 32. Jean-Baptiste Thiers (1636-1703) -- co-author (with Lebrun) of the Superstitions material; Traite des superstitions (text); volume 2 frontispiece after Sebastien Le Clerc. Proposed as extra verified spare for #545. 33. Bruzen de la Martiniere (dates NOT established) -- wrote Ceremonies text with J.-F. Bernard. Held back as a candidate: no dates established. 34. Ezekiel Spanheim, Baron de Spanheim (1629-1710, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- translator of Les Cesars de l'empereur Julien (1728, 300+ medals engraved by Picart). 35. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) -- collaborated with Picart 1729. Already carried as #542 reserve -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed. 36. Gilliam van der Gouwen (c.1657 Antwerp - buried 15 March 1716, Amsterdam) -- Flemish engraver; engraved 41 of the 214 picture-bible plates; teacher of Jan Wandelaar (second-order); engraved many of Philip Tideman's designs (second-order). Proposed as extra verified spare for #545. 37. Jean-Marc Nattier (17 March 1685 - 7 November 1766) -- painted Picart's portrait (GAMEO dates the painting 1709; Wikipedia/NGA date the Verkolje mezzotint after it 1715 -- FLAG the 1709 vs 1715 discrepancy); in Amsterdam 1715, where he painted Peter the Great and Empress Catherine. Dates from Encyclopaedia Britannica via search, not from a page read end to end. Proposed as spare 7 for #545. 38. Nicolaas Verkolje (11 April 1673 - 21 January 1746) -- mezzotint portrait of Picart after Nattier, 1715 (National Gallery of Art, accession 2021.39.5); Dutch painter and mezzotinter; influenced by Antoine Coypel, Gerard de Lairesse, Godfried Schalcken, Gerard Dou, Adriaen van der Werff and Gabriel Metsu; teacher of Jan Matthias Kok, Jan Maurits Quinkhard, Arnout Rentinck and Gerrit Zegelaar. FLAG: spelling varies Nicolaas / Nikolaas. Dates from Wikipedia via search, not from a page read end to end. Proposed as spare 8 for #545. 39. Coenraad van Beuningen (1678-1744) -- Mennonite; house owner, Singel 434 ("de drie Beulingen"). Thin link as recorded in the Picart article; included with that reservation. 40. Abraham Moubach (dates NOT established) -- translated the Ceremonies into Dutch (first Dutch edition 1727, 6 volumes). Held back as a candidate: no dates established. 41. Marcus Tuscher (1705-1751) -- engraver in Stosch's Rome circle; dates search-verified only. Second-order (Stosch circle), counted with that reservation. Proposed as extra verified spare for #545. 42. Johann Joachim Winckelmann (1717-1768) -- catalogued Stosch's gem collection 1760. Dates search-verified only. Second-order (Stosch circle), counted with that reservation. Proposed as extra verified spare for #545.

Pupils: 43. Jacob Folkema (1692-1767) -- pupil; ran with Picart's engraving school. Already queued as #542 spare -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed. 44. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) -- pupil; portrayed Picart posthumously; cooperated with Prosper Marchand. Already queued as #542 alternate -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed. 45. Pieter Tanje (1706-1761) -- pupil; also a pupil of Jacob de Wit. Dates "as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source" per the #541 study file. Already queued in #542 -- counted in the network, NOT re-proposed. 46. Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768) -- pupil. Dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3 (39 lines). FLAG: Wikipedia says he "may have been a student of Bernard Picart" while the Picart article lists him as a pupil and DSI lists Education: Bernard Picart -- the hedging is Wikipedia's, the pupil link is the stronger record. Huguenot origin, settled The Hague; married Jeanne Bouffet 1722; engraving of Vivaldi 1725; illustrated Voltaire's Henriade and Tragedies, Garcilaso de la Vega's Comentarios Reales (Paris ed. 1731), Rapin de Thoyras's History of England vols I-X (1724-27); engravings after Hogarth and Coypel; portraits of Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch; active London c.1731-34 and c.1750; self-portrait 1765; buried Walloon Church. Proposed as main 1 for #545. 47. Johann Philipp Endelich (1700 - buried 18 Sept 1760) -- pupil (RKD, per the Picart article). Portraitist; engravings after his own drawings; one striking pastel. FLAG: spelling Endlich vs Endelich (Jeffares: "Philippus Endlich"); birth 1700 per Jeffares vs unknown per RKD ("?-1760"). Proposed as alternate 1 for #545.

Engraved after: 48. Raphael (1483-1520, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- Impostures innocentes (1734, published by Picart's widow) is engraved after Raphael, Guido Reni, Carlo Maratta, Poussin and Rembrandt. 49. Guido Reni (1575-1642, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- as above. 50. Carlo Maratta (1625-1713, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- as above. 51. Nicolas Poussin (1594-1665, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- as above. 52. Rembrandt van Rijn (1606-1669, as commonly cited, not verified in this run) -- as above; also already carried as a #535 extra verified spare.

The great picture bible -- verified: Figures de la Bible (1720) and Taferelen der voornaamste geschiedenissen van het Oude en Nieuwe Testament (The Hague: Pieter de Hondt, 1728-29): 214 large engravings. Designs: Gerard Hoet 119, Arnold Houbraken 27, Picart 64 (119 + 27 + 64 = 210; four designs unattributed in sources read -- FLAG the arithmetic gap). Engravers included Gilliam van der Gouwen (41 plates), Duflos, Thomassin, Folkema and others. Descriptive text by Jacques Saurin. The 1728 Figures illustrations in the Hoet article (Genesis 1:1, Jacob's Ladder) are credited "illustrated by Gerard Hoet and others".

Sitters: no evidenced portrait sitters of Picart's own engraving work were established in this leg. Picart himself sat for Jean-Marc Nattier (portrait, 1709 per GAMEO / 1715 per Wikipedia), engraved in mezzotint by Nicolaas Verkolje. Menno Simons is shown in a section of the Ceremonies frontispiece (GAMEO) -- a depicted historical figure, not a sitter; not counted.

NOT counted (second-order, named in entries above only): Gilles Rousselet and Guillaume Vallet (Etienne Picart's circle); Gabriel II Martin (Marchand's Paris partner); Peter the Great (visitor to Levinus Vincent's cabinet, 1697; painted by Nattier in Amsterdam 1715); Menno Simons (depicted in the Ceremonies frontispiece); Romeyn de Hooghe (Vincent frontispiece); Jan Wandelaar (Gouwen's pupil); Philip Tideman (Gouwen's source); Pieter Bodart (engraved Hoet's drawing book); Jacques Ignatius de Roore (Hoet's dealer partner); Hendrick Schoock (Hoet's academy co-founder); Warnard van Rijsen and Cornelis van Poelenburgh (Hoet's teachers); Thomas Birch, George Vertue, Jan Wagenaar, Isaac Tirion (Jacobus Houbraken's collaborators); R. Simon, J. Abbadie, Dupin, Boulainvilliers, Reland (Ceremonies text sources); Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch, Vivaldi, Voltaire, Rapin de Thoyras, Garcilaso de la Vega (Morellon la Cave's subjects and authors). Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare): the task brief names him as someone Picart engraved after, but no Picart-de Lairesse link was established from sources read in this leg -- NOT counted as Picart network, and not re-proposed.

Cross-links worth noting: Picart to Marchand to Schley -- the refugee bookseller who fled Paris with Picart, sketched the pictures, and with the pupil arranged the illustrations for numerous books; the book trade as a three-man firm. Picart to Hoet to Houbraken -- the picture bible as a three-way division: Hoet designs 119, Houbraken 27, Picart 64, and the engravers (Gouwen, Duflos, Thomassin, Folkema) cut them all. Picart to Stosch to Winckelmann -- the gem catalogue as a chain: Stosch collects 10,000 stones, Picart engraves 70 of them enlarged, Winckelmann catalogues the collection in 1760, and the classical canon is built on Picart's plates. Picart to Bernard to the world -- the publisher writes ~3,000 pages of text, Picart designs all the engravings, seven students and other engravers execute them, and the result is banned in Rome and reissued bowdlerized in Paris, the same plates carrying both the original and the censored text. Picart to his pupils to Vinkeles -- the school as the institution: Folkema, Schley, Tanje and Morellon la Cave carry the Amsterdam engraving line into the next generation, and the Stadstekenacademie Vinkeles directed for forty-three years is the school's afterlife (see C548 on the unverified descent).

Influence line: Picart's documented influence line runs through the school and the book, not through a single mechanism. His pupils (Folkema, Schley, Tanje, Morellon la Cave, Endelich) extend the line one generation further, and the Amsterdam book trade -- the Huguenot publishers, the picture bible, the Ceremonies as the first comparative survey of world religions -- is the institution he built. No named pupil of Picart is found carrying a particular Picart mechanism forward by name -- the line continues as a school and a catalogue, not as a style. The documented influence is the book itself: the Ceremonies' images "became the standard means of portraying many of the world's religions until well into the nineteenth century", and the Impostures' defence of reproductive engraving is the argument the next generation inherited.

## Future candidates
Top pick for #545 is Jacobus Houbraken (25 December 1698 - 14 November 1780) - the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left. Son of Arnold Houbraken (carried #535 spare); born Dordrecht, learned engraving from his father; moved to Amsterdam 1707 and helped with the Groote Schouburgh (1718-21), finishing the last proofs with his mother after his father's death; influenced by studying Cornelis Cort, Jonas Suyderhoef, Gerard Edelinck and the Visschers; devoted himself almost entirely to portraiture; famous through Heads of Illustrious Persons of Great Britain (1743-52, with historian Thomas Birch and artist George Vertue); 1752-59 worked with historian Jan Wagenaar (1709-1773) on the 21-part Vaderlandsche historie for publisher Isaac Tirion; engraved the portraits for Jan van Gool's Nieuwe Schouburg (Den Haag, 1750-51); his portrait of Nicolaas Verkolje dates ca. 1752; his oeuvre of 400+ portrait engravings, often set in an oval frame with a subtitle stating the subject's claim to fame, is an important record for art provenance. He is the top pick because he is the direct continuation of Picart's line: Picart's picture-bible collaborator, the man who turned the Amsterdam portrait engraving Picart practised into a system -- the engraved face as the only surviving likeness, annotated with its source -- and the bridge from the Amsterdam engraving school to the English portrait series and the Dutch national history. Studying him next keeps the chain inside the workshop Picart built, before it branches to Picart's father, his fellow pupils, or his Paris teachers. The doorway is the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left. Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 39 lines) - the pupil who carried Picart's book-illustration trade into English history publishing. Huguenot engraver, settled The Hague; married Jeanne Bouffet 1722; engraving of Vivaldi 1725; illustrated Voltaire's Henriade and Tragedies, Garcilaso de la Vega's Comentarios Reales (Paris ed. 1731), Rapin de Thoyras's History of England vols I-X (1724-27); engravings after Hogarth and Coypel; portraits of Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch; active London c.1731-34 and c.1750; self-portrait 1765. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the pupil who left the workshop for London, not the collaborator who stayed in it: the trade carried outward, where Houbraken is the line carried forward. The doorway is the pupil who engraved Vivaldi and then illustrated the history of England. Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 185 lines) - the painter-engraver who designed the Bible Picart's shop engraved. 119 of the 214 picture-bible designs are his -- more than Picart's own 64; founded a drawing academy at Utrecht 1697 with Hendrick Schoock; published a book on drawing 1712 (103 prints by Pieter Bodart); source for Houbraken's Bentvueghels list; art dealer with Jacques Ignatius de Roore; trained by his father Moses Hoet and Warnard van Rijsen. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw, where Houbraken is the man who engraved the faces. The doorway is the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw. Etienne Picart le Romain (1632-1721; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the father, and the root of the line. Graveur du roi; Rome 1655-61 with Guillaume Vallet; pupil of Gilles Rousselet (not Francois de Poilly, per the British Museum); Academie Royale member 1664; last years from 1710 in the Netherlands, joining his son in Amsterdam; Bernard's first teacher. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the root before the trunk, not the continuation after it: the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave, where Houbraken is the son who carried the Amsterdam line on. The doorway is the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave. Prosper Marchand (11 March 1678 - 14 June 1756; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the bookseller-bibliographer who fled Paris with Picart and became the book-trade half of his world. Paris shop rue Saint-Jacques under the Phenix (with Gabriel II Martin); The Hague refuge with Picart Jan 1710; abandoned bookselling c.1713; proofreader for Fritsch & Bohm, Rotterdam; editor Journal litteraire 1713-1737; Dictionnaire historique (1758-59, posthumous); London 1726-1735; left his papers to Leiden University Library; with Picart and pupil Jakob van der Schley arranged illustrations for numerous books, supplying descriptions and rough sketches. He is the reserve because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who sketched the pictures Picart engraved, but whose own drawn work is the description, not the plate. The doorway is the refugee bookseller who sketched the pictures Picart engraved. Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses and taught the next engraver in the chain. Wall-covering and theatre-decoration painter; travelled to Brabant with Vinkeles and Izaak Schmidt in 1765; subject, with Schmidt and Vinkeles himself, of the 1765 triple portrait; teacher of Hermanus Numan at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the travelling companion, not the trunk: the painter beside the engraver on the road, whose wall-coverings are the large-scale cousin of Vinkeles's theatre sets. The doorway is the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses -- and taught the next engraver in the chain. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; the 20 "Andriessen" log mentions are all Louis Andriessen (1939-2021), the composer, studied in the Rzewski chain -- never Jurriaan; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates search-verified only (Alamy/WGA captions). Hermanus Numan (1744-1820; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Engraver, copyist, theorist and publisher; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770; travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771; later Felix Meritis like Vinkeles and Vrijdag. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the theorist of the line, not its trunk: the man who wrote about engraving as well as engraving, and whose career runs parallel to Vinkeles's from the same Paris meeting. The doorway is the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn, and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. French engraver; head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris; teacher of Cochin, Cathelin, Strange and Ryland, as well as of Vinkeles for a year in 1770-71; Vinkeles lived in his house and learned his dry-needle technique there. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the French master behind the Dutch pupil: the technique itself, not the Amsterdam line -- and a study of him is a study of Paris, not of the chain that runs through Punt. The doorway is the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn -- and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Engraver; "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles; his 106-print Studiebeelden en Fragmenten (1807-16) is the next generation's drawing book; his 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. He is main 5 rather than top pick because he is the continuation after Vinkeles, not the trunk before him: the pupil whose handling derives from Vinkeles's late manner, and the proof the school outlived the master. The doorway is the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the namesake met in Paris, a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Painter; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770. He is the reserve because he is a near-name collision with Jacob de Wit (study #541) and almost nothing is recorded beyond the Paris meeting -- but the meeting itself is dated and the confusion is worth one study to clear. The doorway is the namesake met in Paris -- a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight, a fellow of the Pax circle. Marine painter and etcher; fellow member of Pax Artium Nutrix with Vinkeles; at the club he "became friendly with prominent collectors such as Jacob de Vos and Cornelis Ploos van Amstel". He is alternate 1 because his life is short and his oeuvre small, but his Pax membership and his marine etchings put him at the centre of the circle Vinkeles drank in. The doorway is the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight -- a fellow of the Pax circle. Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the draughtsman behind the Felix Meritis hall, the drawing the old master engraved. Draughtsman; with Pieter Barbiers II he drew the Felix Meritis drawing hall that Vinkeles and Vrijdag engraved in 1801; also a Barbiers collaborator. He is alternate 2 because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man whose drawing of a room became Vinkeles's largest sheet. The doorway is the draughtsman behind the Felix Meritis hall -- the drawing the old master engraved. Theatre and decorative painter; son of Pieter Barbiers I (carried #542 spare); with Jacques Kuyper he drew the Felix Meritis drawing hall engraved by Vinkeles and Vrijdag in 1801. The doorway is the theatre painter's son who drew the academy hall with Kuyper. Jacob Haafner (1754-1809; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the workshop apprentice who became the travel writer whose Ceylon book Vinkeles illustrated at sixty-five. Writer, not artist; apprenticed in Vinkeles's workshop (introduced by the merchant J. Hilkes), then travelled to India and Ceylon; Vinkeles made the original drawings for the only two illustrations to his Lotgevallen (1806); called the European discovery of India "the biggest disaster this country has been able to face". The doorway is the workshop apprentice who became the travel writer whose Ceylon book Vinkeles illustrated at sixty-five. Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the architect who directed the drawing academy beside Vinkeles and built Felix Meritis. Architect; director of the Amsterdam Stadstekenacademie alongside Vinkeles; designed the Felix Meritis building on the Keizersgracht (with its drawing hall, engraved by Vinkeles in 1801), the Weesp City Hall and the Groningen City Hall; wrote a 1768 pamphlet on competitions for the city drawing academy. The doorway is the architect who directed the drawing academy beside Vinkeles -- and built Felix Meritis. (The 14 names carried in #542 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jacobus Houbraken (25 December 1698 - 14 November 1780), Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) (mains); Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) (reserve); Simon Fokke (1712-1784), Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) (alternates); spares: Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775), Pieter Barbiers I (bapt. 23 April 1717 - 7 September 1780), Jan Baptist Xavery (Antwerp 1697 - 1742, The Hague), Gerard Joseph Xavery (27 July 1700 - after 1747), Jacob Folkema (1692-1767), Jan de Beijer (24 September 1703 - c.1780), Jacob van Huysum (bapt. 25 February 1688 - 1740), Caspar Jacobsz Philips (1732-1789). Jacobus Houbraken (25 December 1698 - 14 November 1780) - the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left. Son of Arnold Houbraken (carried #535 spare); moved to Amsterdam 1707; helped his father with the Groote Schouburgh (1718-21); made 400+ portrait engravings, often the only surviving likenesses of the sitters; Heads of Illustrious Persons of Great Britain (1743-52, with Thomas Birch and George Vertue); engraved for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche historie 1752-59 for Isaac Tirion; portraits for van Gool's Nieuwe Schouburg (1750-51); in his 80th year engraved scenes from Troost's De Ontdekte Schijndeugd; his models were Cornelis Cort, Jonas Suyderhoef, Gerard Edelinck and the Visscher family; also a dealer and collector of Rembrandt etchings. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the portrait engraver, not the academy man: the face as a record, where Vinkeles is the school as a record. The doorway is the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: Amsterdam move 1707 (Wikipedia) vs 1710 (British Museum). NOTE: the #541 file's entry 42 says "(Houbraken already carried as a #535 spare)" -- that refers to his father Arnold Houbraken (1660-1719), carried as a #535 spare, NOT to Jacobus. Jacobus does not appear in the #541 file's Future candidates section at all: the 13 "Houbraken" hits in that file are Jacob as Tanje's teacher (network entries 8 and 42) and Arnold in the carried blocks. Jacobus is proposable; Arnold stays carried and is not re-proposed. Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) - the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Pupil of Norbert van Bloemen and of Jacobus Buys (carried #541 main); married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main); a lumber merchant by trade and a collector of 5,000+ drawings including Rembrandts. He invented the prenttekening -- a transfer/offset method of reproducing drawings in the crayon manner, from 1765; his Hoofdwerk ran to 46 prints at 350 impressions each. His collection sold in 1800 for 109,406 guilders. His pupils included Christina Chalon, William V Prince of Orange, Elisabeth van Woensel and Hermanus Petrus Schouten; he reported Petrus Camper's facial-angle findings; a member of Arte et Amicitia. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the collector's answer to the engraver's question: not the man who made the prints, but the man who figured out how to print the drawings themselves, and the proof that the print trade had a second half Punt never touched. The doorway is the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: one source gives "Print made by: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel in 1559-1760" -- an obvious typo for 1759-1760. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) - the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. History painter who became an engraver; pupil of Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare), Gerrit Rademaker (1672-1711) and Jacob van Huysum (proposed spare 7); sexton of the Amstelkerk from 1718; cooperated with Picart in 1729; made the title plate for van Gool's Nieuw Schouburg (1750); a central figure of the Stadstekenacademie circle (Folkema, Morellon, Troost, Fokke, Elgersma, Tanje); works at the Westerkerk, Nieuwe Kerk and Town Hall; "exchanged brush for graver"; his collection was auctioned in 1776; Ploos ranked him with Isaac de Moucheron, de Wit and Troost. He is the reserve because he is the hinge between the painter's trade and the engraver's: the man who made the same crossing Punt made in reverse (Punt went from needle to brush late; Dubourg went from brush to graver), but his own engraved oeuvre is thin and his dates rest on one article. The doorway is the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Simon Fokke (1712-1784) - the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Pupil of J. C. Philips (proposed spare 1); the booksellers' engraver of small portraits and vignettes; plates for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche Historie 1749-59; Danish portraits for Tycho Hofman (1741); after Vernet, Avercamp, Terborch, Ribera and Troost (a burlesque Death of Dido); the title vignette for Rousseau's Discourse on Inequality (1755); his portrait was painted by John Greenwood after Jacobus Buys. The doorway is the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) - the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Pupil of Picart from about age 12; completed unfinished plates after Picart's death; made the memorial portrait of Picart (1734); maps for Prevost's Histoire generale des voyages (publisher Pieter de Hondt, The Hague); illustrations for La Vie de Marianne (1735-47); Brantome frontispieces (1740); L'Imprimerie (1739); taught Philippus van der Schley (1724-1817); cooperated with Prosper Marchand. The doorway is the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Jacobus Buys (19 November 1724 - 7 April 1801; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Painter and engraver; studied under Cornelis Pronk, Jacob de Wit and Cornelis Troost; director of the Amsterdam Drawing Academy; joined the Guild of St Luke in 1750; bas-reliefs, tapestry designs, book illustrations and theatre scenes. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the pupil line after the engraver: the man who learned the witje, the flower piece and the theatre scene from three different masters and turned them into a teaching post. The doorway is the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Tako Hajo Jelgersma (29 October 1702 - 24 March 1795; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years, and outlived him by forty-one. Painter; de Wit's follower in grisaille; pupil of Frans Decker and Wigerus Vitringa; moved to Haarlem in 1752; pupils Johan Bernard Brandhoff, Johannes Petrus van Horstok, Cornelis van Noorde, Martinus van der Jagt and Johannes Swertner; lived to 92. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the influence line itself, narrow and documented: the only named man who kept painting grisailles in de Wit's manner after de Wit was gone, and the proof that the witje outlived its inventor. The doorway is the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years -- and outlived him by forty-one. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left. German-born Dutch painter, draughtsman, calligrapher and printmaker, the leading Dutch marine painter after both Willem van de Veldes left for England in 1672. He came to Amsterdam around 1650 as a merchant's clerk, taught himself to draw ships, studied under Allaert van Everdingen and Hendrick Dubbels, joined the painters' guild in 1663, and became famous for violent water - he went out to sea in an open boat to study storms. He painted the great View of Amsterdam and the IJ (1665) as a present for Louis XIV's minister, opened a gallery on the top floor of the Amsterdam town hall in 1699, and was visited by Cosimo III de' Medici and Peter the Great. His pupils and followers include Jan Claesz Rietschoof, Aernout Smit, Michiel Maddersteg and Abraham Storck. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the hub of the whole marine spare set - three of this study's spares are his pupils or followers - so studying him unlocks all of them, and his open-boat storm study is a mechanism no other candidate offers. The doorway is the clerk-turned-calligrapher who rowed out into storms in an open boat to learn how water breaks, and then taught half of Amsterdam's marine painters. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries.
