(The 26 names carried in #530 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Adam Elsheimer (Frankfurt, baptised 18 March 1578 - Rome 11 December 1610; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - German painter in Rome, master of multi-source night light on copper. Elsheimer arrived in Rome in early 1600, the year Caravaggio's revolution broke, and answered it not with tenebrism but with up to five gentle light sources on a copper plate the size of a hand - moonlight, torchlight, firelight and starlight in one picture. His Flight into Egypt (1609) is one of the first realistic night skies in European painting. He influenced Rubens, Rembrandt, Claude Lorrain, Paul Bril, Jan Pynas, Leonaert Bramer and Pieter Lastman, and through Lastman the whole Amsterdam history school - the light-line that runs parallel to Baburen's dark-line and meets it again in Rembrandt. He died at 32, the same age as Baburen, and his friend Rubens mourned him in print. The doorway is the other northerner in Rome while Baburen was still a boy in Utrecht, and the one whose light Baburen never learned. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Jusepe de Ribera (Xativa, baptised 17 February 1591 - Naples 1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Spanish painter, "Lo Spagnoletto", the chief visual source for Baburen's saints. Cussida commissioned Ribera's Five Senses in 1615-16 while he was commissioning Baburen's Pieta Chapel, and Franits makes Ribera the chief visual source for Baburen's Archimedes, St. Francis and Pan Holding a Syrinx - the hard, sculptural, plebeian body that Baburen brought home to Utrecht. Ribera then did what Baburen never lived to do: he stayed, married into Neapolitan painting, ran the city for thirty years under the viceroys, etched forty plates, and pushed tenebrism to its most brutal extreme in martyrdoms and flayings. The doorway is the Spaniard who shared Baburen's patron and taught him his saints. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: death date disputed (2 September vs 3 November 1652); Ribalta pupillage undocumented, Giovanni Serodine (Ascona 1600 - Rome 21 December 1630; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Ticinese Caravaggist in Rome. Serodine's Saint Michael was painted for San Pietro in Montorio, the same church where Baburen and de Haen decorated the Pieta Chapel, and his Christ among the Doctors (Louvre) and Jesus and the Tribute Money (National Gallery of Scotland) are the most violently brushed tenebrist canvases of the 1620s Roman scene. He was the son of Ticinese stucco workers, trained by his elder brother Giacomo, made few friends and patrons in Rome (Baglione), produced only about fifteen surviving paintings, and died at 30 - the same short fuse as Baburen, the same city, the same decade, the opposite temperament: loose, luminous, provincial, eccentric where Baburen is monumental and composed. Revived by Roberto Longhi; a 2022 dossier exhibition in Modena centred on his Santo scrivente. The doorway is the Caravaggist Baburen never met but shared a church with. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Johannes Vermeer (Delft, baptised 31 October 1632 - 15 December 1675; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch painter, the long echo of the Procuress. Baburen's Procuress hung in Maria Thins's house, and Vermeer painted it into the background of The Concert (c. 1664) and Lady Seated at a Virginal (c. 1670), and may have taken his own Procuress (1656) from it - the one object, the Essential Vermeer site notes, that materially links us to Vermeer's world besides his own 37 paintings. A Vermeer study after Baburen is the long echo: the brothel scene Baburen staged as a loud, cropped, candlelit transaction becomes, forty years later, a small framed picture on a quiet wall, its moral noise turned into pictorial meaning. The doorway is the painting on the wall. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; one whole-log mention only, inside the Baburen material; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. NOTE FOR THE PARENT: Vermeer is the biggest name in this list; hold or promote him at the parent's discretion. Reserve: Bartolomeo Cavarozzi (Viterbo, born 15 February 1587 - Rome 21 September 1625; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Italian Caravaggist who carried the style to Spain the way Baburen carried it to Utrecht. Taken into the Palazzo Crescenzi before 1617 by his patron Giovanni Battista Crescenzi, he travelled with him to Madrid in 1617, worked on the Escorial, and returned to Rome in 1619 - the exact years Baburen was painting the Pieta Chapel a few streets away. His work is the lyrical, still-life-haunted side of the movement (Virgin and Child, Sacrifice of Isaac, Saint Jerome with Two Angels, 1617), recently revalued after decades of neglect; Gianni Papi's 2015 monograph is the first and only one as of 2019. The doorway is the Italian who carried Caravaggism to Spain the way Baburen carried it to Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: birth year sometimes given as 1586; the Guercino-training claim is an error (Cavarozzi was four years older than Guercino). Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Herman van Swanevelt (Woerden 1603 - Paris 1655; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch pastoral landscapist, Bentvueghel (bent-name "heremiet", the hermit) who reached Rome in 1629. Baburen's Granida and Daifilo (1623) was the first painting of Hooft's play and started the Dutch pastoral vogue; Swanevelt made the pastoral landscape itself the subject - sunlit contrejours, shepherds at a ford, no biblical or mythological excuse at all - and with Pieter van Laer and Claude Lorrain brought the Italianate landscape into its classical phase. He was Claude's lodger-companion and model, and the intermediary through whom Claude's art reached the north. The doorway is the pastoral after Baburen's pastoral. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: teacher uncertain (a new hypothesis says Willem Buytewech), Claude Lorrain, born Claude Gellee (Chamagne, Duchy of Lorraine, c. 1600 or 1604/5 - Rome 23 November 1682; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - French landscapist, the sun to Baburen's candle. Claude arrived in Rome around 1617, ground colours for Agostino Tassi, studied under Goffredo Wals in Naples, and by the late 1630s was the leading landscapist in Italy - the one painter of the Roman scene who put the sun itself, low and streaming, into the picture. He learned from Elsheimer's foliage and Breenbergh's example, sketched the Campagna with Poussin, and recorded every finished painting in his Liber Veritatis (British Museum) as an anti-forgery ledger. The doorway is the sun to Baburen's candle. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: birth year disputed: his tombstone says 1600, contemporary sources say c. 1604/05. NOTE: Claude is a very large subject; he works as an alternate precisely because the lane may want the smaller Swanevelt first. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 8): Gerard Douffet (Liege 1594 - 1660/61) - the Liege Caravaggist who knew Baburen in Rome and ended his career in Utrecht. Hold: his ground overlaps Valentin and Manfredi; use if a Liege turn opens. Paulus Bor (Amersfoort c. 1601 - 10 August 1669) - Bentvueghels founder with Baburen ("Orlando"); returned 1626, decorated Honselaarsdijk with Jacob van Campen; his style drifted from Caravaggism to a strange, primitive classicism; friends drank a barrel of wine at his funeral. Hold: better held for a Bentvueghels-family follow-on. Bartholomeus Breenbergh (Deventer, baptised 13 November 1598 - Amsterdam, buried 5 October 1657) - Bentvueghels founder ("het fret"); Rome 1619-1630 with Paul Bril; influenced Claude Lorrain; teachers Pieter Lastman and Jacob Symonsz Pynas. Hold: his fame is landscape, a step sideways from the candlelight line. Orazio Borgianni (Rome 6 April 1574 - 14 January 1616) - Roman Caravaggist, Spanish years 1598-1605, principal patron the Spanish ambassador Francisco Ruiz de Castro; friend of Carlo Saraceni and Antiveduto Grammatica; accused with Saraceni of complicity in an assassination attempt on Giovanni Baglione allegedly planned by Caravaggio; threw a bottle of varnish at Caravaggio in the street. Monographic exhibition Palazzo Barberini 2020. Hold: Italian batch with Saraceni and Gentileschi. Antiveduto Grammatica (Rome 1569 - April 1626) - the head-painter ("gran Capocciante") in whose studio the young Caravaggio worked on arrival in Rome; shared patrons Del Monte, Giustiniani and the Mattei with him; Principe of the Accademia di San Luca 1624, disgraced for trying to sell its Raphael altarpiece. Hold: Italian batch. WARNING: birth given as Siena 1571 in older sources; Petrucci (Paragone, 2004) established Rome 1569. Pieter Lastman (Amsterdam 1583 - buried 4 April 1633) - the man who taught Rembrandt; in Rome 1602-1607, friend of Elsheimer and Rubens; his history paintings (David and Uriah, Coriolanus) laid the foundation of Amsterdam history painting. His pupils included Rembrandt, Jan Lievens and Breenbergh. Hold: big figure, needs a full study slot of its own; doorway runs through Elsheimer. Paul Bril (Antwerp 1553 or 1554 - Rome 7 October 1626) - the Flemish landscape patriarch of Rome; papal commissions from Gregory XIII and Clement VIII; friend of Elsheimer; teacher of Agostino Tassi; precursor of Poelenburgh, Breenbergh and Claude. Principe of the Accademia di San Luca 1620. Hold: landscape line, pair with Breenbergh. Claude Vignon (Tours 19 May 1593 - Paris 10 May 1670) - French Caravaggist; in Rome c. 1609-1617 with Simon Vouet and Valentin de Boulogne; absorbed Manfredi, Fetti and Elsheimer; later worked for Richelieu and Louis XIII; admitted to the Academie royale 1651. Hold: French batch with Valentin. Giovanni Lanfranco (Parma 26 January 1582 - Rome 30 November 1647) - Carracci pupil (Agostino, then Annibale); dome of Sant'Andrea della Valle (1625-27) and the Gesu Nuovo and San Gennaro chapel in Naples; the illusionistic ceiling painter who carried Correggio's dome language into the High Baroque. Rivalry with Domenichino, Reni and Albani for Roman patronage. Hold: ceiling-painting turn. Roelant Savery (Kortrijk 1576 - buried Utrecht 25 February 1639) - court painter to Rudolf II in Prague, with access to the imperial zoo; the most prolific painter of the dodo (ten depictions, 1611-1628) and a pioneer of the independent flower still life; returned to Utrecht 1618, died bankrupt. Nephew Hans (Jan) Savery continued the dodo pictures. Hold: his fame is natural history, a step sideways from the candlelight line. Cornelis Saftleven (Gorinchem c. 1607 - Rotterdam 1 June 1681) - the versatile younger Utrechter: farmhouse interiors, hell scenes, animal satires, proverb illustrations; van Dyck drew his portrait for the Iconographia as "Hollandus Pictor Noctium Phantasmatum" (Dutch painter of nightly and fantastic scenes); Rubens owned eight of his works. Hold: a generation younger; use for a later-Utrecht turn. Jan Mijtens, also Johannes Mytens (The Hague c. 1614 - 24 December 1670) - the Hague portraitist who carried the Granida line forward: his Meeting of Granida and Daifilo (c. 1655-60, Rijksmuseum) is one of 32 known Dutch paintings of the subject; among the leading Hague portraitists with Honthorst, Adriaen Hanneman and Pieter Nason; co-founder of the Confrerie Pictura (1656). Hold: portrait line, pair with Hanneman. NAME TRAP: Houbraken confused him with the Brussels-born Johannes Meyssens. Jacob Adriaensz. Backer (Harlingen 1608 - Amsterdam 27 August 1651) - Amsterdam's "Rembrandt's opposite": about 140 paintings in twenty years, pioneer of Amsterdam classicism, Granida and Daifilo c. 1635 (Hermitage) and the huge Crowning of Mirtillo (1641); the supposed Rembrandt pupillage is a myth debunked by Blankert and Broos. Hold: Amsterdam classicism turn. NAME TRAP: not Jacob de Backer (c. 1540-c. 1591), the Flemish Mannerist. Giuseppe Vermiglio (Milan c. 1587 - Turin, after 1635) - Milanese Caravaggist; exhibited beside Serodine in the 2022 Modena dossier show; dates and career thinly documented - thin-evidence flag, propose only if the lane wants a deep-cut Roman. Bertholet Flemalle (1614-1675; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - Douffet's only known pupil; carried the Liege Caravaggist line a generation on. Hold: thin record; pair with Douffet. Daniel Mijtens (Delft c. 1590 - The Hague 1647/48; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - uncle of Jan Mijtens, portrait painter at the Stuart court in London; the Mijtens family thread. Hold: family follow-on after Jan Mijtens. Jacob van Campen (Haarlem 1596 - Amersfoort 1657; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - architect-painter, Bor's collaborator at Honselaarsdijk; the classicising turn after Caravaggism. Hold: architecture turn. Cornelis Schut (Antwerp 1597 - 1655; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - the sixth name in the c. 1620 Bentvueghels drawing; Flemish history painter, Rubens circle. Hold: thin record in this leg. Jan Linsen (dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - Paulus Bor's particular friend among the Bentvueghels (bent-name "Hermaphrodit"); almost nothing else established in the sources read - thin-evidence flag.)

(The 9 names carried in #529 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis van Poelenburgh (1594/5 - 12 August 1667; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Italianate landscape painter of Utrecht, Bloemaert pupil, co-founder of the Bentvueghels in Rome in 1621 with Jan van Bijlert. He painted the Seven Children of the King and Queen of Bohemia (1628) for Charles I, was himself invited to London by Charles I in 1637 and stayed until 1641, collaborating there with Alexander Keirincx; his small cabinet landscapes with nymphs and ruins were the Utrecht alternative to Honthorst's candlelight. The doorway is the same road to London: the Bloemaert pupil who painted the same Bohemian sitters as Honthorst and then took the same invitation from the same king, nine years later - and the Utrecht painter Rubens actually visited in 1627, while ter Brugghen went unmentioned. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Jan van Bijlert (1597/98 - November 1671; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch painter, the fourth Utrecht Caravaggist named in the Honthorst article. Bloemaert pupil; co-founder of the Bentvueghels in Rome 1621 with Poelenburgh; some 200 pictures, the most prolific of the group; dean of the Utrecht Guild 1632-1637; painted genre, portrait and religious works, moving from Caravaggism to classicism. The doorway is the fourth man: the most prolific of the Utrecht Caravaggisti, who outlived the other three by forty years and watched the style he helped found become the past - and who painted the same Calling of St. Matthew a decade later. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: a Venus and Adonis in the Centraal Museum is attributed to Honthorst where it is "incorrectly attributed to Jan van Bijlert" per Dayton - the misattribution runs both ways between Honthorst and Bijlert; a study should note it, Joachim Wtewael (1566 - 1 August 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Mannerist painter of Utrecht, the founding generation Honthorst grew up beside. Trained with Joos de Beer, who also trained Bloemaert; stayed Mannerist while the Caravaggisti arrived around him; Honthorst "must also have been extremely familiar with the work of Joachim Wtewael" (Sotheby's). The doorway is the Mannerist who stayed: the Utrecht master Honthorst learned to see against, painting small, jewel-like mythologies while the candlelight painters took the big rooms - and the Utrecht master whose brother rented ter Brugghen his house. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Georges de La Tour (13 March 1593 - 30 January 1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - French candlelight painter of Lorraine. His Caravaggism "probably reached him through the Dutch Caravaggisti of the Utrecht School" and he "is often compared to the Dutch painter Hendrick Terbrugghen" (Wikipedia); his night scenes - the Magdalen, the Newborn Child, the Cheat with the Ace of Clubs - take the Utrecht single-flame grammar into French religious painting, quieter, stiller, and more severe. The doorway is the daylight-to-night transfer: the Utrecht idiom carried into France, where the candle burns slower and the dark gets deeper - and the Frenchman who learned Caravaggio through the Dutch. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. NAME TRAP - FLAG IN THE STUDY FILE: this is Georges de La Tour of Vic-sur-Seille, the candlelight painter, NOT Maurice-Quentin de La Tour (1704-1788), the pastellist already studied as #507. Reserve: Matthias Stom (Stomer, c.1600 - after 1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch or Flemish candlelight painter, the specialist of the covered candle. His half-figures with a covered candle or other hidden light source create dramatic chiaroscuro directly after Honthorst, "Gherardo delle Notti"; worked in Rome, Naples and Sicily, then Malta. The doorway is the covered flame: the painter who hid the light source itself and let only its effect show, the Honthorst grammar taken one step further into darkness - and the northern Caravaggist who never came home, Rome, Naples, Sicily, and 250 pictures. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: his pupillage under Honthorst is contested - the MFAH Houston bulletin calls him Honthorst's most successful student, Wikipedia says no documentary evidence exists and Christie's suggests training under ter Brugghen instead (see C383 in #528 and C417 here); do not state the Honthorst pupillage as fact, Adam de Coster (c.1586 - 4 May 1643; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter of Antwerp, the "Pictor Noctium" (Painter of Nights). His half-length candlelight singers - A Man Singing by Candlelight, A Boy Singing by Candlelight - show the Utrecht idiom carried to Antwerp without any recorded Italian trip, "includ[ing] Gerrit van Honthorst" among his models (National Gallery of Ireland). The doorway is the night painter who never went to Rome: the Utrecht candle carried to Antwerp by pictures alone, not by travel - and Antwerp's "Painter of Nights", who took the Utrecht half-length and turned out the lamps. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. NOTE: birth year varies by source (Wikipedia gives c. 1586, born 1585 or 1586), Bartolomeo Manfredi (1582 - 12 December 1622; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Italian painter, Caravaggio's most important follower in Rome. Honthorst's interest in light and shade was "at one with Bartolomeo Manfredi" (Gerson Digital Italy), and his 'Manfrediana methodus' - half-length figures, actual size - was taken up by Baburen, Honthorst and ter Brugghen. The doorway is the Roman who taught the Utrecht men their method: the teacher none of them sat under, whose pictures were the school - and the Roman who taught the north how to paint low life, the transmitter between Caravaggio and Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. Alternates if any of the 3 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Valentin de Boulogne (1591 - 19 August 1632; christened 3 January 1592, probably born late 1591; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - French Caravaggist in Rome. Baburen in Italy "generally adhered more closely to the strong chiaroscuro of Caravaggio's mature style, as did Valentin de Boulogne" (Gerson Digital Italy); Honthorst's Denial of St Peter (c. 1623) "reveals the influence of the French Caravaggesque artist Valentin de Boullogne" (Dayton). The doorway is the Frenchman in Rome: the third foreigner in the Roman circle, whose chiaroscuro Baburen followed and Honthorst borrowed - and the Frenchman in Rome at the same moment, drinking in the same taverns and painting the same cheats. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: evidence in #528's leg was thinner than for the other five alternates (two citations); the parent may prefer to swap in a spare, Gerard Seghers (1591 - 1651; the Manfredi article gives 1589-1651 - flag the discrepancy in the study file; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish Caravaggist, in Rome c. 1613-1620, pupil of Manfredi, then the most successful history painter in Antwerp after Rubens and van Dyck, sliding from tenebrism into a grander Rubensian manner - the arc from Caravaggio to Rubens in one career. The doorway is Manfredi's pupil, who carried the Roman lesson to Antwerp and then outgrew it. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. PROMOTED from a #528 spare (see C417 note). Spares (verified, NOT part of the 10): Simon Vouet (9 January 1590 - 30 June 1649) - French, in Italy 1613-1627, president of the Accademia di San Luca 1624, then Premier peintre du Roi to Louis XIII; the teacher of Valentin and the man who carried Italian Baroque into France. Hold: big figure, needs a full study slot of its own; doorway runs through Valentin. Jan Janssens (1590-1650) - Ghent Caravaggist; his lost Baburen-derived Annunciation survives only through his copy, and his work parallels Stom's - the Ghent branch of the network. Hold: thin English-language literature; pair with a Ghent/Flemish batch later. Theodoor Rombouts (1597-1637) - Antwerp Caravaggist, named with Stom as one of "the most penetrating of Caravaggio's imitators". Hold: his ground overlaps Stom and de Coster; use if an Antwerp turn opens. Hendrick de Somer (1602-1656) - Lokeren-born, Naples-based Caravaggist, pupil of Ribera; the Neapolitan end of the Flemish network. Hold: pair with Stom in a Naples batch. Abraham Janssens I (c. 1571/75-1632) - Antwerp history painter, in Rome 1598-1601, Caravaggesque before Rubens's return; the older Flemish generation. Hold: prequel figure, needs care with the Janssens/Janssens namesakes. Theodoor van Loon (1581-1649) - Mechelen/Brussels Caravaggist, worked for the Archdukes in Brussels; the courtly Flemish branch. Hold: thin literature in English. Carlo Saraceni (1579-1620) - Venetian in Rome, early Caravaggist, teacher of Jean LeClerc; already in ter Brugghen's influence list. Hold: Italian batch with Gentileschi. Orazio Gentileschi (1563-1639) - early Caravaggist, father of Artemisia, later court painter to Charles I; already in ter Brugghen's influence list. Hold: Italian batch; note Artemisia as a possible later study in her own right, not as an appendage. David de Haen (c.1585-1622) - Baburen's companion and housemate in Rome, Giustiniani patron. Held back (carried from #528): thin record; better held for a Baburen follow-on. Jan Both (c.1618-1652) - Italianate landscapist, pupil per Sandrart. Held back (carried from #528): his fame is landscape, a step sideways from the candlelight line. Jan Gerritsz. van Bronckhorst (Bronchorst) - Honthorst pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record. Robert van Voerst (1597-c.1636) - Honthorst pupil, Van Dyck's London engraver. Held back (carried from #528): his fame is engraving, not painting. Gerard van Kuijll (1604-73) - Honthorst pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record. The Le Nain brothers (Antoine, Louis, Mathieu, 1593/1607-1648/1677) - French Caravaggists named in the La Tour cluster. Held back (carried from #528): three painters, one study would need to choose. Trophime Bigot (1579-1650) - French candlelight painter. Held back (carried from #528): not researched in this leg, hold unverified. Gerard de Lairesse (1640-1711) - later Utrecht classicist. Held back (carried from #528): not researched in this leg, hold unverified. Nicolas Tournier (1590-1639) - Manfredi pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record in this leg. Nicolas Regnier (1591-1667) - Manfredi pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record in this leg. Angelo Caroselli (1585-1652) - Italian Caravaggesque painter, a common source for the Utrecht St Sebastian group. Held back: Italian batch with Saraceni and Gentileschi. Caravaggio (Michelangelo Merisi da Caravaggio, 1571-1610) - the root of the whole chain. Held back: no study header mentions him in the log, but he was not researched as a candidate in this leg; hold for the parent's decision rather than proposing him as an ordinary hop.)

(The 10 names carried in #527 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jan Anthonisz. van Ravesteyn (c.1572 - buried 21 June 1657; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch court portraitist of The Hague, Mierevelt's follower and his direct rival for the same commissions. Follower of the Delft portrait painter Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt; member of the Guild of St Luke of The Hague 1598; his workshop produced many portraits for the House of Nassau and competed with Mierevelt for several commissions; known for schutterstukken and for the meticulous rendering of costume in muted colour; teacher of a long pupil list including Adriaen Hanneman (disputed - see C335); his daughter Marie married Hanneman in 1640; in 1656 he left the guild with dissatisfied colleagues and co-founded the Confrerie Pictura; his portrait was painted by Van Dyck. The doorway is the rival in the next town: the Hague portraitist who learned Mierevelt's manner, fought him for the Orange commissions for fifty years, and then broke the guild his master had joined. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. RECORD WARNING: birth given as c.1572 (Wikipedia) against 1570 (auction houses); his pupillage under Mierevelt is unproven - Wikipedia states "it is unknown who taught him", and the pupil claim rests on stylistic inference (see C336), Paulus Moreelse (1571 - 6 March 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch portrait painter of Utrecht, Mierevelt's pupil who built the second centre. Pupil of the Delft portrait painter Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt for about two years, then travelled in Italy; back in Utrecht by 1596, member of the saddlers' guild (which then embraced painters); in 1611, with Abraham Bloemaert, co-founder of Utrecht's new painters' guild (St Luke's), its first deken; a well-known portrait painter receiving commissions from across the Dutch Republic; also architect (Catharijnepoort, Utrecht, 1626), city treasurer and council member; taught at Utrecht's tekenacademie, his pupils including Dirck van Baburen; among the first Dutch painters of Arcadian shepherds and shepherdesses. The doorway is the pupil who left: Mierevelt's Delft portraiture carried to Utrecht, where it founded a guild, a school and a pastoral genre Mierevelt never touched. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. Reserve: Pieter Mierevelt (5 October 1596 - 11 January 1623; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder son of the subject, the painter who could pass for his father. According to Houbraken, one of two sons of Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt who died early, "whose works were such that they could easily pass for his father's"; Houbraken found him through a 1620 portrait signed with an entwined P and M at the home of the Frisian painter Gerard Wigmana; according to the RKD he painted the Anatomy Lesson of Dr Willem van der Meer (the only person looking at the doctor is Pieter himself). Died in Delft aged 27; his brother Jan died young in 1633. The doorway is the bloodline and the attribution problem in one person: the son whose hand cannot be told from the father's, and whose one firm work is an anatomy lesson - the genre his father's rival's pupil would also paint. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: thin record - one firm work (the Anatomy Lesson) and Houbraken's anecdote; a study would lean on the workshop/attribution problem rather than an oeuvre. If it fails, fall to the alternates. Alternates if any of the 3 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Anthonie van Montfoort, called Blocklandt (1533 or 1534 - 18 October 1583; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Mannerist painter, Mierevelt's teacher and the founder of Utrecht Mannerism. Born in Montfoort (his father was at one time mayor); pupil of Hendrick Sweersz in Delft and of Frans Floris in Antwerp (Floris is carried as a #526 alternate - the chain continues); settled in Delft, painting for the Oude Kerk and Nieuwe Kerk, works later lost to the beeldenstorm; trip to Italy 1572; settled in Utrecht for good, joining the guild in 1577; with Joos de Beer, responsible for the Mannerist style begun by Utrecht artists around 1590; painted biblical scenes, mythological subjects and portraits; best-known work Joseph interprets Pharaoh's Dream (Centraal Museum, Utrecht); the Assumption triptych of 1579 (Bingen am Rhein); his oil sketches of 1574 were engraved by Philip Galle (carried #525 spare). The doorway is the teacher: the Mannerist master whose school Mierevelt entered at fifteen, and whose Italianate history painting Mierevelt abandoned for the portraiture that made him rich. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: many works lost to the beeldenstorm; small surviving oeuvre, Anthonie Palamedesz. (1601/02 - 27 November 1673; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Delft portrait and genre painter, Mierevelt's pupil who turned the portrait into the merry company. Born in Leith, Scotland, son of the sculptor/gem-cutter Palamedes Willemsz. Stevens; taught by Mierevelt and the history painter Hans Jordaens; entered the Delft Guild of St Luke in 1621 (or 1636 - sources conflict) and was its hoofdman (deacon) in 1635, 1658, 1663 and 1672; best known for gezelschapje (merry company) scenes of elegant figures at music, play and conversation, and for guardroom scenes; a major figure in the development of genre painting in Delft; his pupils included his younger brother Palamedes Palamedesz. I, the battle painter, and Ludolf de Jongh. The doorway is the pupil who left the single face behind: Mierevelt's portraiture opened out into rooms full of people talking, playing and drinking. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. RECORD WARNING: birth given as Delft 1601 (dealers) against baptised 9 November 1602 in South Leith (Wikipedia, citing the baptism record); guild entry 1621 against 1636, Hieronymus Wierix (1553 - 1 November 1619; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish engraver of Antwerp, Mierevelt's first master. Son of the painter/cabinetmaker Anton Wierix I; a child prodigy who began engraving at 11 (1565) and made excellent copies after Durer a year later; worked for the Plantin Press (first paid 1570); admitted to the Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1572/3; known for reproductive engravings after Durer and other masters and for delicate religious prints on a very small scale; brothers Jan (1549-c.1618) and Anton II (c.1552-c.1604) were engravers too; his apprentices included Abraham van Merlen, Jan Baptist van den Sande the elder and Jacob de Weert; "the portraitist Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt began his training with Hieronymus, although he soon moved to another master" (Wierix family article). The doorway is the burin line Mierevelt started with: the boy who could write calligraphy at eight learned the engraver's line before he ever painted a face, and the print is what carried his portraits across Europe afterwards. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526, Thomas de Keyser (1596/97 - 1667; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam portrait painter, the city's leading portraitist before Rembrandt arrived in 1631/32. Second son of Hendrick de Keyser (1565-1621), Amsterdam's famed architect, sculptor and municipal stonemason; trained as a mason under his father from 1616; likely studied painting with Cornelis van der Voort (RKD; British Museum: "Likely studied with the portraitist Cornelis van der Voort"); most important portrait painter in Amsterdam prior to Rembrandt; in the 1620s introduced small-scale full-length sitters in interiors, combining the court portrait format with everyday objects; Constantijn Huygens and his Clerk, 1627 (National Gallery, London); The Company of Captain Allaert Cloeck and Lieutenant Jacobsz. Rotgans, 1632 (Rijksmuseum); from 1640 ran a basalt business, returning to painting in 1654; city architect of Amsterdam 1662-1667. The doorway is the Amsterdam machine against the Delft/Hague one - and Huygens, Mierevelt's sitter and champion, sits to de Keyser too, clerk at his side. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: the van der Voort pupillage is "likely" (RKD/British Museum), not documented (see C344); birth 1596 against 1596/97, Cornelis van der Voort (c.1576 - buried 2 November 1624; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam portrait painter, inventor of the regentenstuk. Born Antwerp, son of a cloth weaver; family moved to Amsterdam c.1585 as Calvinist refugees; painted individual portraits and group portraits including schuttersstukken and regentenstukken depicting regents of charitable institutions, a genre of which he was the inventor; introduced the life-size, full-length format to Dutch portraiture; also an art collector, appraiser and dealer; likely teacher of Thomas de Keyser. The doorway is the civic portrait against the court portrait: where Mierevelt painted princes for the Orange court, van der Voort invented the group portrait of the men who ran Amsterdam's charities - the republican portrait, life-size and in the room. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: his teachers (Aert Pietersz, Cornelis Ketel) rest on low-quality sources only (see C343); Ketel himself is already carried (#525 reserve), Adriaen Hanneman (c.1603/04 - buried 11 July 1671; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - The Hague portraitist, the Ravesteyn pupil who did go to London and came back a Van Dyckian. According to guild records dated 1619 he studied with Anthony van Ravesteyn II (c.1580-1669), younger brother of Jan (the Leiden Collection, 2024) - though Wikipedia lists him among Jan van Ravesteyn's pupils (see C335); in 1626 he moved to England, where he may have worked in the studios of Daniel Mijtens (carried #524) and of Van Dyck (carried #524), who arrived in London in 1632; married Elizabeth Wilson in London 1630; returned to The Hague c.1638 amid the growing unrest; painted Constantijn Huygens and his five children, 1640 (Mauritshuis); married Maria van Ravesteyn, Jan's daughter, in 1640; painted Charles, Prince of Wales, c.1648 (original lost, about 30 copies); catered to the exiled Stuart court in The Hague; first dean of the Confrerie Pictura. The doorway is the second generation of the refused invitation: the Hague pupil who went to the London Mierevelt refused, learned Van Dyck there, and brought the Van Dyck manner home to paint the exiled Stuarts. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. RECORD WARNING: teacher disputed between the Ravesteyn brothers (guild records say Anthony II; Wikipedia and dealers say Jan); birth c.1603 against c.1604; London arrival 1623 (Wikipedia) against 1626 (Leiden Collection). Spares (verified, NOT part of the 10): Jan Mierevelt (1604-1633) - younger son. Held back: the whole record is "died young in 1633" plus workshop membership; no work, no dates beyond the death year; a study now would be invention. Use only if the Pieter reserve fails and the study can be built from the workshop evidence. Willem Jacobsz. Delff (c.1580 - 14 April 1638) - engraver, son-in-law, Mierevelt's exclusive reproductive engraver; married Geertruid 1618; Ekkart: his portrait prints "are among the best of their type ever made in Holland". Held back: his oeuvre is reproductive - the print after the painting, not a painted oeuvre; better held for a print-line study after a painting study. RECORD WARNING: born c.October 1580 / died 14 April 1638 (Wikipedia) against born 19 November 1580 / died 11 April 1638 (genealogieonline). Willem van Honthorst (1594-1666) - younger brother of Gerard; pupil of Abraham Bloemaert and of Gerard; Berlin court painter to Louise Henriette of Nassau 1647-1664; many of his works were previously misattributed to Gerard through the similarity of their signatures. Held back: the brother-shadow; better held for a Honthorst-family follow-on after Gerard is studied. Johan Dircksz. van Nes (died 1650, Delft) - pupil per Houbraken/RKD; teacher of Christiaen van Couwenbergh; one named work. Held back: thin record, no birth date. Hendrick Cornelisz. van Vliet (1611/12 - buried 28 October 1675) - pupil; portraits to c.1650, then Delft church interiors (Oude Kerk, Nieuwe Kerk; Pieterskerk, Leiden 1652). Held back: his fame is architectural painting, a step sideways from the portrait line; hold for a Delft architectural-painting turn. Willem van der Vliet (c.1583/84-1642) - Delft portraitist; uncle/teacher of Hendrick, pupil of Mierevelt. Held back: thin record; overlaps Hendrick. Jacob Willemsz Delff the Elder (c.1550-1601) - Delft portraitist; the fellow townsman whose style Mierevelt first followed; father of Willem Jacobsz. Held back: his record overlaps the Delff family story and the workshop; use if the study series takes a Delft-family turn. Abraham Bloemaert (1566-1651) - Utrecht master; teacher of Honthorst, co-founder of the Utrecht guild with Moreelse. Held back: second-degree link to Mierevelt (teacher of his pupil's contemporary, guild partner of his pupil); a big figure in his own right - hold for a Utrecht-school turn, not as a Mierevelt-network pick. Joachim von Sandrart (1606-1688) - Honthorst's pupil c.1625-1628; author of the Teutsche Academie, a principal source for this whole period. Held back: writer-painter whose fame is the treatise and the source value, not a large painted oeuvre in this line. Palamedes Palamedesz. I (1605-1638) - battle and cavalry painter; younger brother of Anthonie, his pupil; died young. Held back: thin record; better held for a Palamedesz-family follow-on after Anthonie is studied. Jacob Willemsz. Delff the Younger (1619-1661) - grandson; took over Mierevelt's workshop. Held back: thin record; the workshop story belongs inside the #527 study. Anthonie / Anthony van Ravesteyn II (fl. 1614-1669) - younger brother of Jan; primarily a glass painter; Hanneman's teacher per the 1619 guild records. Held back: glass painter, thin record; his value is the Hanneman-teacher correction (C335). Jan Daemen Cool - "possibly" a pupil (Kalden). Held back: one uncertain line, dates not verified in this run. Pieter Gerritsz. Montfoort - "his favourite pupil", an orphan (Kalden). Held back: one line, no dates or works found in this run. Klaudius Kornelisze - pupil named by Kalden. Held back: name only.)

(The 10 names carried in #526 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jean Bahuet (c.1552-1597; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish court painter to Vincenzo I Gonzaga in Mantua; Pourbus's direct predecessor, post vacant 1597-1600 until Pourbus arrived. He painted almost exclusively idealised female portraits, which the duke presented in his "Gallery of Beauties"; documented in Florence from 1575, settled Mantua 1579, on the ducal payroll at 30 lire a month plus materials. Most of his works are lost; no signed work is known; his oeuvre is being reconstructed from the Gallery of Beauties group, the Vincenzo state portraits and the Mantuan account books. The doorway is the chair Pourbus took: the post stood empty for three years before Pourbus filled it, and the two men's Vincenzo portraits bracket the succession. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. WARNING: thin record, most works lost, Frans Pourbus the Elder (1545-1581; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - father of the subject; portrait and religious painter of Antwerp, some genre scenes; trained first with his father Pieter, then in Frans Floris's Antwerp workshop from 1564; master of the Guild of St Luke in Bruges and Antwerp 1569; married Suzanna Floris in 1566, died of typhoid fever 19 September 1581 aged 36, when the Younger was 12. His mature work possibly shows Anthonis Mor's influence (Mor sought his help finding apprentices in 1572). The doorway is the father: the Antwerp training and the Floris workshop the Younger inherited, and the painter whose death at 36 left a 12-year-old to carry the dynasty. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. NOTE: listed among #525's exclusions as a family member; proposed here on his own record as a painter (see C330). Reserve: Pieter Pourbus (c.1523-1584; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - grandfather of the subject; Bruges portraitist and painter-cartographer; the leading portrait and history painter in Bruges in the second half of the 16th century; 27 cartographic commissions 1549-1583, 7 maps surviving; the painted Map of the Liberty of Bruges (1571) is his magnum opus; teacher of the Elder and (probably) of the Younger; pupil of Lancelot Blondeel by marriage. The doorway is the dynasty's root: painter and mapmaker in one hand, and the master who most likely trained the Younger. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. Alternates if any of the 3 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Gortzius Geldorp (1553-c.1619; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - fellow pupil of Frans the Elder alongside the Younger; left Antwerp c.1579 in the service of Carlo d'Aragona Tagliavia, Duke of Terranova, Philip II's ambassador, and stayed in Cologne, becoming the leading portrait painter of his generation there and the local elite's favorite portraitist; about 70 works attributed, mostly portraits; Cologne painters' guild representative Christmas 1609; Cologne city council 1610 and 1613. The doorway is the fork: same training, civic career instead of court career. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. RECORD WARNING: death year 1618 in older sources, c.1619 in recent scholarship, Frans Floris (1519/20-1570; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the "Flemish Raphael", leading Antwerp Romanist; master of the Elder, great-uncle by marriage of the Younger; his workshop was the Italianate school the Pourbus line came out of; on his death his unfinished grand-prior commission was completed by his assistants Frans Pourbus the Elder and Chrispijn van den Broeck. The doorway is the master behind the father. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. RECORD WARNING: birth year 17 April 1519 per the EN Wikipedia infobox, 1517 per Alchetron, Lancelot Blondeel (1498-1561; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Bruges painter, architect, surveyor, cartographer; Pieter's teacher and father-in-law, the Younger's great-great-grandfather by marriage; Bruges' most renowned artist when Pieter arrived in 1543. The doorway is the Bruges root: the Renaissance polymath the dynasty married into. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525, Adriaen Thomasz Key (c.1544-after 1589; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Antwerp portraitist; took over Willem Key's workshop 1567; his portraits were long misattributed to Mor and to Frans Pourbus the Elder (one 1574 portrait was successively catalogued as Mor, then Pourbus the Elder, before Key's monogram was revealed on cleaning); Mor's influence visible in his later work; called "the most talented of his generation" by David Freedberg. The doorway is the lookalike: the Antwerp contemporary whose pictures cannot be told from the Elder's. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525, Jan van Scorel (1495-1562; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Utrecht painter, Mor's teacher (Mor was his apprentice, then assistant by 1540); painter-cartographer like Pieter Pourbus (both named in the "Mapping the wet land" study of painter-cartographers 1480-1550). The doorway is the teacher of the teacher: Mor's master, and the Utrecht link in the cartographer-painter line. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525, Hubertus Goltzius (1526-1583; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - painter, engraver, antiquarian; subject of Mor's last attributed portrait (1576); his son Hendrick Goltzius is already carried (#525 alternate). The doorway is the sitter who was also a maker: Mor's last portrait, and the father of the carried engraver. The doorway is the Cologne predecessor: the seat Geldorp inherited. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. Melchior Geldorp (fl. 1620-40) - son of Gortzius, also painted history and portraits. Held back: thin record; better held for a Geldorp-family follow-on after Gortzius is studied. Cornelis Floris de Vriendt (1514-1575) - sculptor/architect, the Younger's maternal grandfather. Held back: a sculptor, not a painter, and his name collides with the already-carried "Cornelis Floris II"; hold unless the study series takes a sculpture turn.)

(The 11 names carried in #525 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Wenceslas Hollar (1607-1677; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Bohemian engraver, etcher, and painter, born Vaclav Hollar in Prague. Ruined by the Sack of Prague, he left law for art, apprenticed to Matthaus Merian in Frankfurt in 1627, and was taken up by Thomas Howard, 21st Earl of Arundel - Daniel Mytens' first English patron - travelling with him to England in 1637. He spent most of his life in England, producing some 2,740 plates and 400 drawings: city views (the Long View of London, 1647), maps, costume books, natural-history plates of shells, muffs, and insects, and portraits after Holbein and van Dyck. He stood the siege of Basing House with Inigo Jones, worked in Antwerp 1644-1652, returned to London, and died in poverty in 1677. The doorway is the face itself: the only surviving image of Gheeraerts is Hollar's 1644 engraving after Gheeraerts' lost 1627 self-portrait, so the study of Hollar is the study of how Gheeraerts is remembered at all. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt was carried in #525 and is now studied as #527; he is NOT carried here - see Do NOT re-pick. Reserve: Cornelis Ketel (1548-1616; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Mannerist painter, born out of wedlock in Gouda, apprenticed to his uncle at 11. Driven out by the Dutch Revolt and the 1573 plague, he arrived in London in 1573 and worked there until 1581, painting Sir Christopher Hatton, the Earl of Oxford, the German Hansa merchants of the Steelyard, and - in 1578 - Elizabeth I herself, who sat to him at Hanworth. He then returned to Amsterdam, where in 1588 he painted The Company of Captain Rosecrans, the earliest Dutch group portrait with figures standing full-length, and from 1595 he sculpted as well as painted and wrote poetry. The doorway is the earlier exile: Ketel is the previous generation of the exact emigre line Gheeraerts belongs to - a Netherlandish Protestant portraitist working the Tudor court a decade before Gheeraerts' earliest signed works - and his full-length group portrait is the Dutch cousin of Gheeraerts' full-length court portrait. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Marcus Gheeraerts III (c.1602-c.1654; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder surviving son of Marcus Gheeraerts the Younger and Magdalena de Critz, baptised at the Dutch Church, Austin Friars, and "also a painter" - the third Marcus in a three-generation painting family. That is nearly the whole record: no signed work, no patron, no documented commission was found in this run. His value as a study is the dynasty question - what a court painter's son inherits, a workshop, a name, and a clientele, and whether the name alone sustains a career after the father's fashion fades. The doorway is the bloodline: he is the literal continuation of Gheeraerts' workshop into the Caroline era, and the study would test how much of a famous father's practice survives in a son with no surviving work. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524. WARNING: thin record - a study may need to be built from the family and workshop evidence rather than from his own works, Hendrick Goltzius (1558-1617; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - German-born Dutch printmaker, draftsman, and painter, the leading Dutch engraver of Northern Mannerism. Burned as a child, his right hand permanently damaged, he nevertheless perfected the swelling burin line; he moved to Haarlem in 1577, set up his own print publishing business around 1578, and produced some 388 plates plus about 500 drawings and 50 paintings. His workshop engraved Spranger's designs for Rudolf II's Prague court, and his 1593-94 Meisterstiche imitated Durer, Lucas van Leyden, Raphael, and Parmigianino in their own manners. In 1600 he gave up printmaking and painted. The doorway is the print standard: Goltzius was Galle's pupil and the master engraver of the Northern world the Elder Gheeraerts' prints circulated in, and the English engravers who carried Gheeraerts' sitters into print - the de Passes, then Hollar - all worked in his shadow. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Simon de Passe (c.1595-1647; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch engraver, eldest son of Crispijn van de Passe the Elder. He settled in London in 1616 and built a successful portrait-engraving practice, contributing to Henry Holland's Baziliologia (1618), engraving the royal family and noblemen, making James I's silver portrait medallions (1616-1619), and producing the famous 1616 print of Pocahontas. In 1624 he moved to Copenhagen as royal engraver to Christian IV of Denmark - Anne of Denmark's brother - and stayed until his death. The NPG associates him with 199 portraits. The doorway is the printed court: de Passe engraved the same royal family Gheeraerts painted in oils, in the cheaper medium that actually spread their faces, and his Copenhagen move follows Anne of Denmark's own Danish family line. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, William Segar (c.1554-1633; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English portrait painter and officer of arms, trained as a scrivener, admitted to the College of Arms in 1585, Somerset Herald 1589, Norroy King of Arms 1593, Garter Principal King of Arms 1607-1633. He painted Elizabeth's favourite Essex in black armour for the 1590 Accession Day tilt, is sometimes credited with the Ermine Portrait of Elizabeth, and painted Leicester, Drake, and other courtiers; his last recorded payment as a painter is a portrait of the queen in 1597. He also wrote The Booke of Honour and Armes (1590) and Honor Military and Civill (1602), the literature of the tilt yard. The doorway is the tilt: Segar painted the same Essex and the same Elizabeth as Gheeraerts, and wrote the books that explained the Accession Day pageantry Lee devised and Gheeraerts illustrated - painter, herald, and author of one court spectacle. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Hieronymus Custodis (died c.1593; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish portrait painter, native of Antwerp, one of the Protestant exiles who fled to England after the fall of Antwerp in 1585. Three signed and dated portraits of 1589 - Giles Brydges, 3rd Baron Chandos, and his 14-year-old daughter Elizabeth Brydges, maid of honour to Elizabeth I - firmly establish him in London by 1589; Strong attributes a Sir Henry Bromley of 1587 and an Edward Talbot of 1586 to him. He was a member of the Dutch Church, Austin Friars, and his widow remarried in 1593, the presumed year of his death. His surviving work is small: half-length figures on dark grounds, linear precision in ruffs and jewels, cool colours, meticulous flesh. The doorway is the fellow exile: Custodis arrived in London in the same Antwerp exodus that shaped Gheeraerts' world, painted the same Protestant nobility in the same years Gheeraerts was finding his feet, and died just as Gheeraerts became fashionable - the road not taken, cut short. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Crispijn van de Passe the Elder (c.1564-1637; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch publisher and engraver, founder of the van de Passe dynasty of engravers, comparable to the Wierix and Sadeler families. His Cologne publishing house produced portraits of European nobility; his works include the famous 1605 engraving of the Gunpowder Plotters and a 1598 engraved portrait of James VI of Scotland. Four of his children - Simon, Crispijn II, Willem, and Magdalena - were also engravers, and the family's Heroologia Anglica (1620) holds 65 portraits of English notables. The doorway is the family press: Crispijn is the father of Simon de Passe, who engraved Gheeraerts' royal sitters in London, and his own James VI print of 1598 carried the future James I's face before Gheeraerts ever painted him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, John Bettes the Younger (died 1616; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English portrait painter, son of John Bettes the Elder (active c.1531-1570, painter to Henry VIII and Catherine Parr). Like Isaac Oliver and Rowland Lockey, he is believed to have studied under Nicholas Hilliard. He lived on Grub Street, St Giles Cripplegate, later St Gregory-by-St Paul's; a miniature of Francis Walsingham is attributed to him, and his studio is thought to have made a number of portraits of Elizabeth I. Signed works are very rare - a Portrait of a Lady signed and dated 1589, a girl of 1587. He died in January 1616, bequeathing his son two easels and a porphyry slab and muller for grinding pigments. The doorway is the Hilliard line: Bettes is a fellow pupil of Hilliard alongside Gheeraerts' brother-in-law Oliver, painting the same queen from the same training, with almost nothing signed to prove it - the anonymous end of the Elizabethan portrait trade. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 11): Ferdinando Clifton (active 1627) - Gheeraerts' apprentice, freeman of the Painter-Stainers' Company in 1627. Held back because the entire record is one line: no work, no dates, no further trace found; a study now would be invention. Aert Mytens (c.1541-1601) - uncle of Daniel Mytens, first known Mytens painter; worked in Naples 1574-1592 for the Viceroy's court. Held back because his whole career is Italian, one step removed from the English court line; better held for a Mytens-family study after Daniel Mytens (carried #524) is done. Philip Galle (1537-1612) - Antwerp publisher who printed after Marcus Gheeraerts the Elder's designs; Goltzius was his pupil. Held back because his link is to the Elder, not the Younger, and his ground overlaps Goltzius heavily; hold until the Elder himself is studied (carried as reserve in #522), when Galle becomes the natural print-publisher follow-on.)

(The 10 names carried in #524 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: William Larkin (early 1580s - 1619; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English portrait painter, active 1609-1619, known until the 1950s only as the "Curtain Master" for the voluminous silk curtains in his pictures. About 40 portraits identified, of Jacobean courtiers and gentry, including Richard Sackville, 3rd Earl of Dorset (1613), the twin Cecil sisters (1615), and the nine enormous Suffolk Collection full-lengths at Kenwood; he never painted members of the royal family. His earliest portraits (Edward Herbert, 1609-10, and Sir Thomas Lucy III) had formerly been assumed to be the work of Isaac Oliver until Lees-Milne identified Larkin in 1952, and the ODNB judges his style points to training in one of the London studios - of Isaac Oliver or Marcus Gheeraerts. His work marks the last stage of the Holbein-Hilliard flat portrait tradition, with sitters in meticulously rendered wardrobe and props; he died in 1619, the same year as Hilliard and Robert Peake the Elder, and Apollo calls those three deaths the end of the insular Tudor-Jacobean portrait tradition. The doorway is the Curtain Master whose first pictures were taken for Oliver's, who may have learned his trade in Oliver's own studio, and who closed the flat-portrait tradition Oliver stood in the middle of. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. CAVEAT: the studio training is a stylistic inference, not a document, and it could have been Gheeraerts' studio, Daniel Mytens (c.1590-1647; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch portrait painter (Daniel Mijtens). Anne of Denmark commissioned him alongside Paul van Somer and Isaac Oliver, the three names in one sentence in her biography, as the artists who "led English taste in visual arts for a generation". He arrived in England by 1613, lured by van Somer's success, to whom he was related and whose neighbour he was in St Martin's Lane; he became van Somer's official successor after his death, was granted a pension in 1624, and became Charles I's picture-drawer in ordinary by patent of 30 May 1625, painting 15 portraits of Charles. He is the Dutchman who took the Jacobean court portrait from van Somer and held it until Van Dyck arrived. The doorway is the Dutch successor who shared Anne's patronage with Oliver and van Somer, then held Charles I's portrait until Van Dyck took it from him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. Reserve: Alexander Cooper (baptised 1609 - 1660; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source; RECORD WARNING: death 1660 is the commonly cited year, not verified in this run) - Miniaturist, younger brother of Samuel Cooper, adopted with him by their uncle John Hoskins. Sandrart states he "was taught by Peter Oliver (c.1594-1647) and was 'by far [his] most celebrated pupil'" - the direct teaching link from Isaac Oliver's son to the next generation. He became limner to Christina, Queen of Sweden, and painted a series of portraits of the children of the King and Queen of Bohemia; his Portrait of Elizabeth Stuart, Queen of Bohemia, is in the 2013 Cooper catalogue. He is the pupil who carried the Oliver miniature out of England into a northern European court. The doorway is the pupil Peter Oliver called his most celebrated, who took the Oliver miniature to the court of Christina of Sweden. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Lucas de Heere (1534 - 29 August 1584; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter, watercolourist, poet and biographer, pupil of his father Jan de Heere in Ghent; Protestant refugee, in England c.1567-1577, elder of the Dutch stranger church at Austin Friars. He trained John de Critz, probably Marcus Gheeraerts the Younger, and possibly Robert Peake the Elder - the teacher behind the whole Jacobean workshop web that Oliver worked inside. His pupil Karel van Mander is the source for Lucas Horenbout teaching Holbein. He painted the earliest known realistic depiction of Stonehenge (1573-1575, British Library watercolour) and a gallery of national costumes for the Earl of Lincoln in 1570. The doorway is the Ghent master who taught de Critz, Gheeraerts and perhaps Peake, the teacher behind the four Jacobean workshops. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, David des Granges (baptised 1611 - died in or before c.1672; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source; RECORD WARNING: birth baptised 1611 here against 1613 in Foster's table; death "in or before c.1672" against 1675 in the 1911 Britannica) - Anglo-French miniature painter, initially influenced as a miniaturist by John Hoskins and Peter Oliver. He worked also as an engraver and in oils; was with Charles II in Scotland in the early 1650s copying a likeness of Charles by Adriaen Hanneman; after the Restoration he was influenced by Jacob Huysmans and possibly Samuel Cooper. His Saltonstall Family is in the Tate; work at Ham House and Windsor Castle. He is the next generation of the miniature after Peter Oliver, carrying the Hoskins-Oliver influence through the Civil War into the Restoration. The doorway is the Anglo-French miniaturist Hoskins and Peter Oliver shaped, who carried their miniature through the Civil War to the Restoration court. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Cornelius Johnson (1593-1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Portrait painter, known in the Netherlands as Cornelis Jonson van Ceulen, born in Britain to Flemish parents. "Charles I's other portrait painter" (The Art Newspaper, on Karen Hearn's book): appointed "his Majesty's servant in ye quality of Picture Drawer" in 1632, the first British painter regularly to sign his work, and "almost a worthy rival to his contemporary Van Dyck". He painted three small full-length portraits of Charles I's eldest children in 1639, and his popular portrait miniatures were painted in oil on metal - the miniature carried into oil, the direction Oliver's chiaroscuro had pointed. He left for the northern Netherlands in 1643 after the outbreak of civil war and died in Utrecht. The doorway is Charles I's other portrait painter, who took the miniature into oil on metal and signed his work when no English painter did. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Richard Gibson (1615-1690; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source; RECORD WARNING: birth 1615 in Cumberland is the anecdotal date; it has recently been proposed he was born in London in 1605 to the picturemaker John Gibson) - Portrait miniaturist and courtier, "Dwarf Gibson" for his 3 foot 10 inch stature. Apprenticed to Francis Cleyn at the Mortlake Tapestry Works; entered the service of Philip Herbert, 4th Earl of Pembroke, in 1639. In May 1672 he was re-elected to the court as the king's limner under Charles II, two weeks after the death of Samuel Cooper - "To succeed Samuel Cooper was no light feat". He was succeeded the following year by Nicholas Dixon, and later became drawing-master to the two young daughters of James, Duke of York. His daughter Susannah-Penelope Rosse imitated Cooper's work. The doorway is the dwarf limner who took the king's limner post from Cooper's line two weeks after Cooper died. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Anthony van Dyck (1599-1641; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish Baroque painter, leading court painter in England, dominant influence on English portraiture for 150 years. His first task on arriving in England in 1620 was copying Paul van Somer's royal portraits - copying the man who had displaced Oliver's brother-in-law as Anne's favourite. Peter Oliver made miniature copies after Van Dyck, including the Countess of Southampton, "one of the most remarkable works in miniature existing". Samuel Cooper was "commonly stil'd the Van-Dyck in little", learned his art from Van Dyck's works, and copied Van Dyck's oil portraits, some under his supervision. The doorway is the Fleming whose portraits the Olivers copied in little and whose manner Cooper made the miniature's own, the man who ended the world Oliver worked in. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. STATUS CAVEAT: one whole-log mention exists, a #513/#514 Veronese-influence note listing Van Dyck as "second-order influence, held for a later Veronese-influence leg" - a held note, not a carried candidacy in any New-from #520-#523 block, Francis Cleyn (c.1582-1658; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - German-born painter and designer at the Mortlake Tapestry Works, and a miniaturist in his own right (Foster groups him among the 17th-century miniaturists). He is the master of Richard Gibson: Gibson was apprenticed to him at Mortlake after his talent for drawing was noted while he was a page in a noble household there. The link to Oliver is second-degree, through Gibson and the Mortlake circle, and is flagged as such; Cleyn's own miniature work is the direct art-historical link. The doorway is the Mortlake designer-miniaturist who taught Dwarf Gibson his trade. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Inigo Jones (1573-1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Architect and stage designer, the designer of the Jacobean court spectacle Oliver's sitters performed in: the Barriers devised by Ben Jonson and designed by Jones, performed on Twelfth Night 1610 in the Banqueting House at Whitehall, is cited in the Royal Collection catalogue entry for Oliver's Henry, Prince of Wales miniature, and Jones designed Anne of Denmark's masques, including The Masque of Blackness (1605). His built work - the Banqueting House, the Queen's House at Greenwich - is the architecture of the court Oliver painted for. The doorway is the architect of the masques and palaces Oliver's sitters performed in, the designer of the Jacobean court itself. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions (the local-file grep hit "tony-ray-jones" is a different person, a photographer); no local file; not carried in #520-#523. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 11): Rowland Lockey (c.1565-1616) - painter, goldsmith and miniaturist; apprenticed to Hilliard for eight years from 1581, alongside Oliver; copyist of Holbein's Family of Sir Thomas More; joint beneficiary with Oliver of Peter Matheeusen's 1588 will. Held back because his known work is mostly copies, so a study would be a study of copying as a practice rather than of an original oeuvre. Laurence Hilliard (baptised 5 March 1582 - 1648) - miniaturist, son and pupil of Nicholas Hilliard; royal limner after his father's death; took over the Gutter Lane workshop Oliver had left as a rival. Held back because his surviving oeuvre is small and was judged a "feeble" version of his father's style. Edward Norgate (1581-1650) - writer, herald and limner; author of Miniatura or The Art of Limning, the treatise that carries Hilliard's teaching; wrote of Oliver's Madonna that "cost him two years [of his life]". Held back because he is a writer-painter whose fame is the treatise, not a large surviving painted oeuvre.)

The doorway is the son who carried his father's wax portrait into the next imperial generation. Nicholas Hilliard himself is NOT re-proposed: he is the subject of #523. Steven van Herwijck is NOT re-proposed: already studied (#522). George van Egmond, Engelken Tols, Wouter van Bijler, Jacobus Fabius, Hillegoent van Alendorp, Sigismund II Augustus, Bona Sforza, Catherine Jagiellon, Isabella Jagiellon, John Sigismund Zapolya, Elizabeth I, William Parr, Elisabeth Brooke, William Herbert, Anne Heneage, Thomas Heneage, Thomas Stanley, Richard Martin, Dorcas Ecclestone, Edmund Withypoll, Maria Dimock, John Dymock, Michel de Castelnau and Jonekin van Herwijck are in this study's network and are NOT offered here: sitters, patrons and family, not makers, or makers with no evidenced standalone record in this run -- judgement exclusions by this study, not carried exclusions. Richard Martin is in this study's network and is NOT offered here: a maker (goldsmith, Master of the Mint) whose record is civic and mint-administrative, and whose portrait medallion is the evidence for his birth date rather than part of a body of artistic work -- judgement exclusion by this study, not a carried exclusion. Giovanni Giacomo Caraglio and Hans Schwarz are in this study's network and are NOT offered here: parallel medallists of the same Polish sitters, but their records in this leg are single-medal attributions, too thin to carry as candidates without further research -- judgement exclusions by this study, not carried exclusions. Hans Reinhart the Elder is in this study's network and is NOT offered here: fully verified as a spare (see network entry 39) but held back because the German medallic line is a step sideways from Herwijck's Antwerp-London axis; use if any of the 11 proposed fails -- judgement exclusion by this study, not a carried exclusion. William Cecil, Charles Utenhove, John Lumley, George Vertue, Victor Tourneur, Bendor Grosvenor, Roy Strong, Edward Town and Elizabeth Goldring are in this study's network and are NOT offered here: scholars, collectors and officials, not makers -- judgement exclusions by this study, not carried exclusions. Robert Brandon is in this study's network and is NOT offered here: a maker (goldsmith, the Queen's jeweller) whose record is administrative and commercial, with no surviving body of signed work identified in this run -- judgement exclusion by this study, not a carried exclusion. Laurence Hilliard and Rowland Lockey are in this study's network and are NOT offered here: both fully verified as spares (see network entries 4 and 5) but held back, Laurence because his surviving oeuvre is small and was judged a feeble version of his father's style, Lockey because his known work is mostly copies; use if any of the 11 proposed fails -- judgement exclusions by this study, not carried exclusions. Marcus Gheeraerts the Younger is in this study's network and is NOT offered here: a major Jacobean portraitist, but this run's 11 slots went to the miniature line and he was not proposed by leg 3; he is neither carried nor excluded and may be proposed by a later leg.
