## Corrections

- C541. The doorway tally. This is the one-hundred-and-sixty-fifth doorway check in a row; the hold tally counts only since #420, so the streak now stands at one hundred and eighteen holds and forty-one non-holds. The #543 study file's tally (117 holds, 40 non-holds, 164 checks) was carried forward correctly.
- C542. The Le Brun prize and the Academie reception. The #543 top-pick paragraph says Picart was "taught by Charles Le Brun" and "received into the Academie royale in 1689". Leg 1 (F2/F17) reports the specialist site flags the prize story as a misunderstanding: Le Brun's last award ceremony was 24 September 1689, Picart is not listed as a prize winner in the Academie documents, and the prize drawing is not known. What is documented is that he was sent to the Academie in 1689 to learn drawing under Sebastien Leclerc; formal reception as a member is not established in any source read. The study corrects both claims in the life section and records the doorway D4 as holding in substance and failing in the letter, and D5 as failing in substance and in letter.
- C543. The Ceremonies counts. The #543 top-pick paragraph gives the Ceremonies as "266 engravings, 10 volumes", 1723-1743. Leg 1 (F8) and leg 2 (flag 2) report the counts disagree across serious sources: plates 243 (Sotheby's) vs "more than 250" (Nellis; UCLA) vs 263 (Zocalo review) vs 266 (EN Wikipedia); volumes seven folio volumes 1723-1737 plus two added by Bernard (Sotheby's) vs "ten volumes of text" (EN Wikipedia) vs seven volumes "with supplements eventually reaching 12 volumes" (GAMEO) vs 11 volumes in-folio 1723-43 (FR Wikipedia). The study carries 266/10 volumes as EN Wikipedia's count for one edition, with the spread recorded here and in the Disputes section, and records the doorway D7 as holding in substance and failing in the letter. The Impostures plate count has the same problem (78 vs 70, 1734 vs 1738) and is recorded the same way.
- C544. The inventory date. The #543 top-pick paragraph says the widow's inventory was "of 12 March 1736". Leg 1 (F11) reports the specialist site, citing Stadsarchief Amsterdam Notaris Ardinois 9126, 347, says "The estate inventory was made up on 17 April 1736". The 12 March date may be a different document (testament? valuation?) or an error; unresolved. The counts (about 400 portfolios, 54 paintings) are consistent across both sources. The study carries 17 April 1736 and records the doorway D10 as holding in substance and failing in the letter.
- C545. The Rijksmuseum 2,000+ figure. The #543 top-pick paragraph says "the Rijksmuseum holds 2,000+ of his works". Leg 1 (F24) and leg 2 (flag 15) report this is Wikipedia's figure ("more than 2,000 works online"); no Rijksmuseum object page was opened directly by any leg and no live count was taken. The study carries the figure as Wikipedia's, with the caveat recorded here and in the Disputes section, and records the doorway D11 as holding in substance and failing in the letter.
- C546. The whole-log mentions. The #543 top-pick paragraph claims "2 whole-log mentions, both in the #542 entry" for Picart. The parent's grep finds five mentions: two in the #542 entry (the Depth line: leg 3 read his EN Wikipedia article end to end; the Future-candidates line) and three in the #543 entry (the Depth line, the Push line naming the #544 top pick, and the Future-candidates line). All five are carries, not studies; the substance (never studied before) holds, the letter (two mentions) fails. Same class as #543 C532 and #542 C519.
- C547. The "New from #544" count and the Le Brun/Nattier #506 note. The #541/#542 convention delivers 15 new names (4 mains + 1 reserve + 2 alternates + 8 spares). This run delivers 14 new names (3 mains + 1 reserve + 2 alternates + 8 spares) because the top pick, Jacobus Houbraken, is carried from #542 (a main there), not new, and only three genuinely new mains came out of Picart's network. Two names in this run's slate (Charles Le Brun, Jean-Marc Nattier) were proposed once before in #506's New block (the pastel chain, log line 4779) but are NOT in the current carried chain #522-#543; they are proposed afresh here on direct Picart links, and that earlier proposal is recorded in leg 3 so the #545 run does not mistake it for a duplication. Beaumarchais's single whole-log mention (in #460's research-notes line) is likewise recorded in leg 3 as an unattached mention, not a study.
- C548. The chain claim, unverified in this leg. The #543 top-pick paragraph calls Picart "the trunk of the Amsterdam engraving line Vinkeles inherited" and says his mechanism "is the direct ancestor of Vinkeles's own trade". Leg 1 (F26) reports no source read in this leg traces the Picart-to-Vinkeles descent (Vinkeles was born 1741, eight years after Picart's death; the link runs through pupils like Van der Schley and Tanje and the Amsterdam school tradition). The study frames the descent as the chain's working link -- the school and the book trade as the institution Vinkeles inherited -- and does not present a sourced teacher-pupil chain from Picart to Vinkeles. The doorway justification is recorded as interpretive and holding in substance.
- C549. The twins and the marriage date. The #543 top-pick paragraph does not mention them, but EN Wikipedia -- the spine source for this study -- says Picart married Anna Vincent "In April 1712" and that in May 1713 the couple "had male twins, who both died within a few weeks". Leg 1 (F4) reports the archival record contradicts both: notice of intended marriage 9 September 1712, marriage 25 September 1712 in Haarlem (Amsterdam Stadsarchief; genealogieonline agrees); and the specialist site documents a single son, Etienne, born 15 May 1713, buried 30 May 1713, with a red-chalk portrait inscribed "Etiene Picart mort a trois jours" (Rijksmuseum RP-T-1951-77). The study follows the archival record and the specialist site on both points, with the dispute recorded here and in the Disputes section.
- C550. The settlement resolution. The #543 top-pick paragraph says Picart "settled in Amsterdam 1711" and flags the 1710 variant. Leg 1 (F7) resolves the discrepancy as geographic, not calendrical: passport 8 January 1710; Hoogstraat, The Hague, 1710-1711; Marchand to Amsterdam December 1710-April 1711; Picart follows; Nes 1711-1712; Singel from May 1713. Sources that say "settled in Amsterdam in 1710" (Encyclopedia.com; Sotheby's; McClintock & Strong) conflate the Hague arrival with the Amsterdam move. The study carries 1711 and records the doorway D6 as holding in substance and in letter.
- C551. The Picart line count. The EN Wikipedia Bernard Picart article is 208 lines as counted by legs 1, 2 and 3 of this run. The #543 top-pick paragraph cites 209 lines (leg 3 of #542) and 208 lines (leg 3 of #543); the 209 is the counting-rule difference already recorded in #543 C540 (highest displayed line number + 1 vs "of N" total). The study cites 208 lines, with both counts on record.
- C552. The Do NOT re-pick list, carried verbatim without silent alteration. The list sits in the carried Future candidates chain (from the #522 block) and ends at Francois Dumont (#505); Picart (#544) does not appear in it and was not added. The list is a record of what was excluded at the time it was written, not a live index.
